# Chapter Summaries

## How to read this file

**This file is a rolling window. It holds the whole of the volume currently being written, one section per batch, and nothing older, and everything older is in `state/archive/chapter-summaries-volumes-01-12.md`, verbatim, with every chapter section exactly as it was here.** The window moved for Volumes 01 to 07 at the Volume 07 close and for Volumes 01 to 08 at the Volume 08 close, **and after that cut this file holds the guide and nothing else.** As of the review of Batch 0004 this file was **1.19 MB across 340 chapters**; it was about 140 KB through Volume 07 and **is now about 4 KB, which is the size a rolling summary is supposed to be.** **Nothing is deleted, only moved, and nothing is renumbered, and a volume is never archived while a close is reading it, so the window moved for Volumes 01 to 07 at the Volume 07 close and not before.**

**Read this guide, then read the section for the volume you are writing, and go back further only when a thread demands it. For the Volume 09 first prompt the useful reading is this guide, `state/current.md`, and `outline/volumes/volume-08-close.md` §§3 and 4, which carry the volume in two tables; the five Volume 08 batch sections and the Volume 08 close section are in `state/archive/chapter-summaries-volumes-01-12.md`.** **The live window is empty and opens on Volume 09; the Volume 01 index and every batch section for Volumes 01 to 08 are in `state/archive/chapter-summaries-volumes-01-07.md` and are reachable by grep, not by reading.** A Volume 08 writer who needs a per-chapter summary of a Volume 07 chapter greps the archive; the summaries are all there under the numbers they always had.

**The same warning as in `state/continuity.md` applies. Do not trust a line-number pointer into a chapter file. Refer to a passage by its chapter and its opening words.**


## The window, at the Volume 13 close

**The window moved forward for Volumes 01 to 12 at the Volume 13 close, and it is the first move in this project that TRIMS the live file as well as copying it.** Every one of the five earlier moves, at the Volume 07, 08, 09, 11 and 12 closes, took an exact copy into `state/archive/` and then left the live file whole, and the live file grew back from the copy point every time. **The consequence of that convention, measured, is this: at the moment this cut was taken the five ledgers stood at 3.06 MB together, `state/continuity.md` at 984 KB holding every volume from Volume 09 forward, `state/open-threads.md` at 764 KB, `state/chapter-summaries.md` at 560 KB, `state/batch-summaries.md` at 468 KB, and `state/current.md` at 246 KB holding ten overlapping "What is true at the close of Chapter N" blocks for Chapter 560 through Chapter 650. That is roughly 780,000 tokens of state for a project whose live instruction is about 25,000 words, and Volume 14 cannot load it. The review of Volume 13 Batch 0005 recorded the arithmetic and called it a blocker, and it is one, and the fix is this cut.**

**Nothing is lost and nothing is renumbered.** `state/archive/chapter-summaries-volumes-01-12.md` is a byte-for-byte copy of this file as it stood immediately before this cut, so the entire pre-cut ledger, guides and all, is recoverable by concatenating the archive back onto the live file at the cut point, and item 412 and thread 412 and chapter 412 and batch 412 all mean exactly what they meant. **The cut moved whole sections only, at section boundaries, and it did not touch a single item number, thread number, chapter number or batch number.** What left the live file is: **`## The window, at the Volume 08 close` and every batch section for Volumes 09, 10, 11 and 12, ending with *Volume 12, closed*.**

**What stayed live, and rule 4 of `state/archive/README.md` is why.** `## Hard canon`, `## Ending constraints` and `## Knowledge control` are global and are in this file now whatever their byte offset, and so are the two canon-clarification sections and `## Initial story threads`, `## Answers to the questions the outline phase had to settle`, `## Final-volume questions` and `## Planning status` in the thread ledger. They are in the archive as well, because the archive is a copy, and a global section in both places is harmless and a global section in neither is not.

**The rule this cut changes, stated so that nobody inherits it by accident.** The old convention was *copy, and leave the live file whole*, and its stated justification was that a copy is safer than a deletion. **That is true and it is also what made the files unusable, and both halves cannot stand.** The new convention is *copy first, verify the copy, then trim the live file*, and the verification is one command: the archive must be byte-identical to the pre-cut live file, which is checked here against git rather than by reading. **The window still does not move in the middle of a volume.** It moved for Volumes 01 to 07 at the Volume 07 close, 01 to 08 at the Volume 08 close, 01 to 09 at the Volume 09 close, 01 to 10 at the Volume 11 close, 01 to 11 at the Volume 12 close, and 01 to 12 at this one, and it will not move again until Volume 14 is closed.

**What a Volume 14 writer reads, in this order, and does not read the rest of:** this guide, `state/current.md`, `outline/volumes/volume-13-close.md` in whole, and the threads this close hands on. Everything older is reachable by grep and is not to be read. **The highest item in this file is the newest, and `state/current.md` carries the figure.**

---

## Volume 13, Batch 0001 — Chapters 601 to 610, "The Cheapest Thing on the List"

**601** — Embermonth 26, YR 320, a Friday and not a market day, the second hour, the room opened on a day it is shut. A carrier lost two days at a ford and brought the band; the copyist of about twenty-six reads the fourth sheet twice and says *it is not a return*. Four hands of writing, no seal, nothing on the back, and **Aren Kest at the top of the first page**. It stands a keeper down for nine days and shuts a road for the same nine days, the matter being under question, and names the road by its two ends. The woman of about twenty-nine says a name at the top is the whole of what makes a direction. The man of about thirty-four with a sheet says it names a district, not a road. The copyist says the second true thing of the day: **then it is a direction, and a direction is obeyed**, and nobody has to, and everybody will, because a world in which nothing with a name at the top is obeyed is a worse world, and that is true. Cost: the copyist's, 214.

**602** — Wolfmonth 2, a Thursday and a market morning, the fifth hour. The hearing stands, said in the street first. **The man of about fifty-eight with a badge refuses to call the sheet false**, because it is false only when somebody can show it false and this room has no way of showing anything false, and put a name at the bottom and it is slander today, leave the name at the top and it is a direction and no county can act. Then the second true thing: **a direction obeyed because of a name and not because of an office can be written by anybody in nine counties with a pen, and in thirty-one years it has never been done, and he wants it on a bare leaf with his name at the top of it.** The copyist will not put his name at the top, having made that point herself in the first week of a month, and writes seven words instead: *the man with the badge said it.* The man of about thirty-four with a sheet asks whether he is to send it and is told that is not the badge's office, and that is exactly why it will be done. Cost: the sheet man's, 232.

**603** — Wolfmonth 6, a Monday, the room shut, the counter under the weigh-house. Two sheets about the same four miles of channel, ten days apart in the writing and three weeks apart in the books, in two different series, and one of them is a lie. **The copyist says the rule is the wall and the wall is what a road is put behind**, so that one bad return cannot be set beside one good return and made to look like a pattern, and here are two of the sheets on it. Nobody in that room is permitted to notice that a road can be two things for three weeks. The woman of about thirty-four who keeps the key says out loud, once, that Aren Kest and the keeper of the room were in the lane together on Saturday and not on Thursday, the hearing stood on both, and nobody has said why, and she is not asking. The two sheets are laid side by side on the counter at the seventh hour, which is not a practice, and go back into two books. Cost: the woman of about twenty-nine's, 198.

**604** — Wolfmonth 11, a Saturday and a market morning, second hour to ninth, the culvert at the town's gate. A cart's off-side wheel, a hand's depth of silt, water across the made stone. A boy of about seventeen fetches the man with the limp and he clears it with about nine barrows and a hook. **Three courses of sill laid in lime with the middle course gone between two clean joints, and a new course laid dry across the bed** by men from the district, three weeks before, on an instruction on a sheet with a name at the top. A man of about fifty who lays stone: *we were told to keep the water off the road and we did.* At about the fourth hour his left palm is warm in the silt and he does not look at it. The only number taken off that street all day is the number of barrows. Cost: his, 245.

**605** — Wolfmonth 16, a Thursday and a market morning. The hearing stands and nobody is seated. **The man of about fifty-eight with a badge asks, in the room, in front of nine people, what roads are open.** Seven are named in plain terms with no figure and no distance, the seventh *and a small one, about which I am going to say only that it is small*. Nobody writes a list, because a list with a name at the top can be argued with and a list without one can be added to and taken from. **He answered for the ferry city and the fruit valley and said out loud that he answered for the two he has been in, and the badge said the true thing: you have not chosen, you have been the last one there, and a man who has been last somewhere cannot be argued with at all.** And he says the limit out loud and names nothing: seven, no eighth, and a thing he has run out of that does not ask his permission. The woman of about thirty-four who keeps the key says she has never once in four and a half years been asked which roads are open. Cost: hers, 249.

**606** — Wolfmonth 19, a Sunday, the ninth open crossing, the second hour, a lime kiln on a slope. **Nell Craddock, 40, runs the kiln alone and has crossed twice in two years and was sent by nobody.** A boy of about eleven gives the whole count straight off: nine open crossings, four shut weeks, the longest eleven days. **She will not be the first to say the number, and the reason is on the page: the first number anybody says out loud about a place is the number that place has for the rest of its life, and she is the one who walks it.** A woman of about sixty has held the far end for about thirty-one years and has never been asked what the road is. The finding arrives in his own mouth: **somebody local has to say it out loud in front of people who will remember, and be wrong if they are wrong, and be believed about everything else for the rest of their life.** He walks a quarter of a mile in front of the sledge and is not thanked. Cost: hers, 182.

**607** — Wolfmonth 23, a Thursday and a market morning. **Garvin Slade, 47, brings a page with seven lines, six marked, and the seventh with nothing against it and not struck out.** He is right about the strain, twice, out loud, and does not hide the cost of being wrong. **The block's truest sentence: a road with a figure on it is a weight; a road with nothing on it is not a light thing, it is a gap, and a gap is not carried by anybody, and a gap goes first, and I have never once in my life had to be cruel to close a gap.** The copyist says she could name the gap inside an hour and will not, because the three series are not derived from one another and making one out of another hands nine counties a single number anybody can check. **Aren Kest says the seventh has a woman on it who runs a lime kiln and a boy of about eleven who can say its whole count, and does not say the rest.** Six marks asked for and none given; the page goes back inside a coat. Cost: Slade's, 217.

**608** — Wolfmonth 26, a Sunday, the room shut, the counter underneath. The man of about thirty-four with a sheet cuts the direction to about nine lines, prints it in a district hand, and posts it on a Friday. **A gatekeeper with about eleven years at the ferry stone reads it out and does not check the name, and the page says why: the man is not in the district and nobody there can say who he is.** The keeper of the road is told at the gate, asked nothing, shown nothing, and goes home for nine days. **Nothing rotted, nobody was late, nobody was hurt, and no wages went anywhere.** The badge says the true thing: *it does not need to be believed, it needs to be delivered, and there is not one person in this county who has done a dishonest thing*, and then says out loud in the lane that he said in a room on the second of a month that it had never been done, and it has now been done, and it cost about nine hundred pence of paper and nobody's livelihood. Cost: the badge's, 260.

**609** — Frostmonth 5, a Tuesday, nine days up. The keeper of the road is back and the road is open and the grass is coming up in the middle of it. **She is not here to complain. She is here to say a number**, and the number is nineteen columns of days-passable with nine days taken out in a hand that is not hers, on a slate in her apron, and it is hers and stays in her apron. **The nine days cost her nothing, and the whole of the matter is that for nine days nobody in about nine houses knew whether she was stood down or had gone home for a bad week**, and the reason there was no arrangement is that the standing down came off a gate and the page has her name nowhere on it. She asks for one thing and is told there is no way to agree it. The badge says *I want you to do nothing, and I have never in thirty-one years wanted a man to do nothing.* Cost: hers, 225.

**610** — Frostmonth 7, a Thursday and a market morning four days off, the tenth open crossing, the sixth hour, the kiln again. She came again with a boy and a rope, and said she knew what the seventh one was and that she was not going to stop him, and **then asked him do you want it, twice, the second time in a working voice with a spade in her hand.** The rider: the second she knows, she can stop bringing him things; and there is a woman nine days off who has been stood down nine days for a page with his name on it and does not know it was his name. **He did not answer, and the chapter says why on the page: the answer he had was a yes, and a yes said on that slope at the sixth hour would have made him the seventh thing on the page she had just described.** The boy of about eleven stands four paces off with a sledgehammer up and will not remember any of it. **Nobody thanked anybody and nobody wrote it down.** Cost: his, 213.

## Volume 13, Batch 0002 — Chapters 611 to 620, "Three Blanks and Three Marks"

**611 — Hearthmonth the first, YR 320, a Sunday, the first and second hour at the counter under the weigh-house, the first of a month, 2,757 words, column 202 pence.** The room is shut and the counter is taking about four hundred and nine parish rolls a month. The man of about fifty-eight with a badge says the hearing stands in the street, says there is no stair open, and says it at a counter so that the counter knows it was said. Garvin Slade comes at the second hour with a page that is not the page: a bigger sheet folded in two, printed twice, with six ruled spaces against six lines and the seventh bare and not struck out. He says what is on it, and the one new sentence is that a mark in a space is not a vote but a statement that you will not stand in the way. The man with the badge asks whether the page names anybody, and the page names six places and one man and cannot be shown false. The copyist of about twenty-six says the thing that turns the morning over: it is not a form, a form has a head that says what the thing is for, and this one's head says who sent it, and it is the first thing in four and a half years she cannot tell you what it is. A woman of about fifty comes four days on a cart and says she would give the man a figure for her own road if he were asking about her own road and he is not. Aren Kest says the seventh can be named and has no figure and that a description is not a weight, and that the man may write down which road it is and it will not be a figure. The copyist writes one question on a bare leaf, *what a page with six ruled spaces and no head is*, and it goes in the drawer under the counter because the room is shut and she will not open a room on a Sunday.

**612 — Hearthmonth the fifth, a Thursday and a market morning, the second to the seventh hour in the room and then the lane, 2,403 words, column 215 pence.** The page comes back after four days with one mark in it, in the space against the waterworks country, put in by a woman who keeps a channel by hand for about twenty-two years. The man of about thirty-four with a sheet says a person can only agree not to stand in the way about a thing they can name in a figure. Then the second fact of the morning: the keeper of the room did not come down to the lane, and the copyist says it out loud and nobody agrees with her, and what she says next is the volume's finding, that nothing in that room depends on the woman who keeps it being in it and three series would go on being kept correctly for a month with her out of the lane, and that she has wanted a room like that since she was about nineteen, and that she is saying it in front of the woman. The man with the sheet then says the block's finding about the page itself: a man who will not put a mark in a space is a man who has refused, and the page has no way to hold a refusal, so the refusal comes back as nothing, and nothing in a ruled space is the same as a space nobody has reached. Aren Kest is in the room to the fifth hour and says a page that cannot be disproved is not a page that is wrong, and that a refusal that comes back as a blank means a road everybody refuses will have been shut by a printing press, and that the page is only dangerous for about nine days and after that it is a piece of paper in a coat. He goes down. She comes up at the seventh hour behind the copyist. They pass in the lane and neither says anything.

**613 — Hearthmonth the seventh, a Saturday and a market morning, the second to the seventh hour, 2,324 words, column 229 pence.** A man of about thirty-one from the road office comes four days on a made road with a letter and no paper entitling him to an answer, because his office has a page going round nine counties with a line on it for a road it cannot find. He asks the three questions a clerk is taught to ask, and is told no three times, and then says he will read his own correct reading of the answer before he puts it in his letter: a rule that keeps three series apart is a rule that says no two returns may be read together, and a page that wants six marks cannot be read any other way, so the rule is a wall in front of the only door there is and the wall is doing the work of the man who wrote the page. Nobody says he is wrong. The copyist is not defended and does not want to be; she says the rule was made for a different reason, that a pattern is a thing a district can be shown and the road will not care which half of it is wrong, and then she says the cost out loud, that four years of correctly kept series is why two warnings about the same four miles of channel sat in two books for three weeks, and that she laid them side by side once on a counter and put them back, and that there is no second practice in the room to replace the one she has just called a blind spot. The page comes back with two more blanks, three of six, and a man from a road office calls them refusals and the man with the sheet says he has three blanks and cannot tell. The badge-man asks whether he is going to fill the fourth head and is told there is no form for it and nobody may rule a fifth.

**614 — Hearthmonth the ninth, a Monday and not a market day, the second to the ninth hour at the culvert under the made road at the town's gate, 2,240 words, column 234 pence.** The frost has been and gone twice. The man of about fifty who lays stone comes at the fifth hour with a hod and says he had about nine days to decide whether to come at all and is glad he said that first. He goes under the arch and finds the course laid dry across the bed is gone, and under it a scour about a foot and a half across and a foot and a half deep at the low side, and the bed below it hard and clean, and the two lime-keyed courses where they always were. He says the middle course is the one that goes, because the top course has the road on it and the bottom course is keyed to the sides, and the middle one has nothing above it that would fall and nothing below it that would hold it, and nobody has ever asked him to look at a middle course. He digs to sound twice, lays three courses in lime, and refuses to lay a fourth, and says so before he starts rather than after, because afterwards you would have had a reason. The boy of about seventeen is paid a penny into his own hand. The man will not have the day written down anywhere, because in about nine years there will be a man who says it was always three courses and he will be right about the three and wrong about everything else. And he says the true thing of the afternoon: the worst job he ever saw was not one where the stone was wrong, it was one where the instruction was right.

**615 — Hearthmonth the twelfth, a Thursday and a market morning, the second to the seventh hour, 2,189 words, column 243 pence.** A man of about thirty-six walks four days with a paper in his coat and asks the room for a figure for the seventh road, because he has read that a road with a figure on it is a weight and a road with nothing on it is a gap and he cannot do anything about it for about nine days. The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room gives the correct answer in three parts: the figure is not the room's to give and not the room's to hold and there is no figure in here for most of the roads people ask her about; a road may be described only by its own answer, and if this room makes one series out of another for one man with a paper then every number it holds becomes a number that man is entitled to; and a figure about that road has to be said out loud by a person who walks it, in the place, in front of people who will remember, by somebody who can be moved off it, and there is nobody in that room who can be moved off it and the room is not going to ask, because the moment a room asks a person for a figure the room becomes the party that wants one. She then says plainly that the page will go round twice while he walks four days, that the correct position is not a comfortable one, and that the road will be shut inside a fortnight and there is nothing anybody in that room can do about it. He asks what to take back and is told to write that he asked, that the room would not give, and that the room was right. The copyist repeats that she could name which of the seven is the gap inside an hour and will not, and nobody defends her. The badge-man says there is no form in nine counties for asking a person for a figure or for refusing to give one, and the only place either has ever been written is a bare leaf in a drawer, and that is not a way of doing things.

**616 — Hearthmonth the fourteenth, a Saturday and a market morning, about the second hour in the lane, 2,381 words, column 251 pence.** The man of about thirty-four with a sheet asks Aren Kest to look at a thing outside, and shows him a copy of the forged page in his own one hand, made at the counter and carried to the district and kept in a satchel for about a month, and tells him in advance that he was not going to keep it and is not going to put it in the drawer upstairs. At the top of the first page, in the place where a name goes on a thing that is going to be acted on, is his own name, in a hand that is not his. He reads it twice. Nothing is at the bottom and nothing is on the back. He says the page is not his and is also nobody's, and the man with the sheet tells him the second half of that: a man who says a page is not his is making a claim, a claim has to be believed, and being believed is a thing you are given and not a thing you do, and there is no form in that room for it. He then says the third thing himself, that about four hundred people hold one true thing about him and about four four hundred hold another and he said no when he was asked whether he would put them straight. Then he names the one thing he could do and says he is not going to do it: he could stand in a lane on a market morning and deny the page in front of nine people, and a man with a limp denying a page in a market is a man telling nine strangers which of the two sets he is in, and he has not asked either set anything. The man with the sheet writes on the back of the copy, unasked, that the man named was shown it on that day and did not say the writing was his. The page has also come back with a second mark, from a man on the coast who keeps his count in tides and who put a mark in because he wants the arithmetic honest and because he is the only person within four days of the gap.

**617 — Hearthmonth the fifteenth, a Sunday, the third to the seventh hour at the counter, 2,206 words, column 262 pence.** Garvin Slade says he has been right about the water and is going to be right about a second, smaller thing: a direction that leaves a room on a Friday and is read at a gate on the Friday has no check in it, and a keeper has not been told and there is nobody to tell her. The fix is one day. The man of about thirty-four with a sheet then says what it costs, which is nothing about any road and the whole of his office, because his office is the day. He says a delayed thing has nobody's name on the delay: the blame moves off the gate onto the room, and the room has a door and a number, and he has a face. The badge-man says a rule needs a head and this room has none and neither has the district, and a rule that is right and has nowhere to go is worth exactly nothing, and this is the first time in thirty-one years he has watched one of them be right. Slade says he knew that when he said it and that nobody in nine counties has ever asked him what it costs. Aren Kest then says the argument out loud, in a working voice, to about four people at a counter: a mark is not a vote, a person can only agree to be held about a thing they can name in a figure, and in about nine days nobody will be able to tell a man who said no from a page nobody got to. He is asked what he would do and he does not answer, and the chapter gives the reason and it is not modesty. He says he is going to go and clear a grating because there are nine barrows of silt on that street and nobody has taken them.

**618 — Hearthmonth the sixteenth, a Monday and not a market morning, the second to the eighth hour on a made road four days down, 2,120 words, column 271 pence.** Ordinary work: a bridge over a ditch with about nine yards of parapet gone on the low side, three men and a boy of about seventeen and a cart and a load of stone, and the wages counted out on the sound parapet into nine hands. A carter carrying the district's paper stops and leaves a page on the parapet, and the man of about fifty who keeps the made road reads it standing up and marks a space in his own line without any speech, and says the reason when asked: because I can be found, because he has a figure in yards, because if he is wrong about anything on that page there is somebody he can be told about it and he can say so to his face, and a mark from anybody who cannot be found is worth nothing at all. A woman of about fifty arrives having walked about nine hundred paces and asks for a ditch, about nine hundred paces of it, nine days of three men, about four hundred pence of a county's money, and she has brought the numbers because he will not take her word for one. He says he will cut it in the next dry week, that there is no hearing and no paper, and that he is cutting it because the water is coming over a made road and the made road is his, and he would have cut it in the thaw about four hundred paces at a time over two years and got it wrong in about nine places. She says the one true thing: a page wants a mark and a ditch wants a spade, and only one of them has a head over it, and the one that has a head over it is going round nine counties. He tells the carter not to write the day down. Nobody thanked anybody.

**619 — Hearthmonth the eighteenth, a Wednesday and not a market morning, the second hour at the gate at the low end of a made road, 3,423 words, column 283 pence.** The man of about fifty-eight with a badge has come four days on his own account because a road he keeps is on a page, and says so first, and refuses to say the rest of it again at a gate. The gatekeeper of about eleven years reads the page out a second time to about nine people who did not hear it the first time, and does not check the name, for the same reason as last time. The woman of about fifty asks what her own line is for and is told that her line is the sixth, that the sixth is to be kept, and that the page says nothing at all about the sixth because the sixth is assumed, and that about nine people at a gate have been asked to agree to shut a road on a page that has never once said in nine counties that the road they are being asked to keep exists. She then puts a mark in the seventh space and says why, in a working voice: an empty space is a road nobody is carrying, and she would rather be argued with than be left alone on a page. She reads her count out loud at the gate: nineteen columns, one for each year, of the days that road was passable out of the days in that year, the last column unfinished, and a nine taken out of it in a hand that is not hers that she has never asked about. She says again that she would like somebody in that county to tell her which way her count runs. The man with the badge says nothing for half an hour and the chapter gives the reason. A carter waiting with his horse yoked asks whether this means a road can now be shut on a count, and she says no, and that a count shuts nothing and is the only thing anybody can be argued with, and that one time is one time and nobody may cite it, and the badge-man says *it is not a practice* and she agrees.
 **The block's fifth beat, added here by the verification and repair pass and in no other chapter.** The chapter already held the setup: a carter asks whether this means a road can be shut on a count, the woman of about fifty says no, the badge-man says then it is not a practice, and she says nobody may cite it. **A woman of about forty-three who keeps a road that is not one of the six is at the same gate.** Her line is the fifth. She is not going to argue with a page whose author is not in her county, and her road will stand aside, and that is the whole of what she came to say. Told the cost, she gives it in a working voice as the rest of it and not nine days, and her people knew before she got there because they walked it with her on a Tuesday, and she will not be argued out of it and will not be praised for it. **The condition: if her name goes in that space she wants the count taken first, by the people who walk it, on the ground, before her line is settled.** Asked whose count, she says ours, and says what her road keeps is the crossings that are open and the weeks the road is shut, and that this has never once been a number of days in anybody's mouth and is not going to be one this morning. **The badge-man says he cannot grant it and is told he was not being asked to, because no head in nine counties can grant it, and the road's own people are asked instead and are not in that county; if they take it the count is theirs and is dated by whoever walked it and not by a gate, and if they will not then her line stays blank, and a blank is a thing a page cannot read.** Nobody wrote it down and the gatekeeper read nothing out, because it was not on the paper. **The woman of about fifty alone explains why none of it is a precedent, which is nobody else's to explain: the road did not agree to be shut, the road agreed to be counted, those are not the same, the page cannot tell them apart, and that is the whole trouble with the page.** The man of about thirty-four with a sheet wrote one line on his own untitled sheet and showed it to nobody, **and did not put it in with the other two things that have gone down that lane in a coat, because what he had was a thing a person had asked for and not a number — a number of barrows, and a note on the back of a copy that went down it on the fifteenth — and a man who has spent a month watching one figure get made out of another figure does not write the two in one line, and that is Chapter 0620's own tally of three left standing and made correct rather than renumbered.** Nobody at that gate was thanked, and she said before she went that she would not be thanked and was not going to stop keeping the count. **The chapter's two coda lists moved with it, distance and count from thirteen to fifteen by the addition of the two categories in the road's own frame with no figure on either, and persons from ten to eleven by the addition at the end of the woman of about forty-three, so that the existing first, second and sixth cross-references are unshifted, with a fourth added for the eleventh.**

**620 — Hearthmonth the nineteenth, a Thursday and a market morning, the second to the seventh hour in the room and then the lane, 2,800 words, column 294 pence.** The keeper of the room did not come down for the second market morning running and the copyist says so out loud again and says she will not say it a third time. The page comes back from nine counties and the chapter sets out exactly what is on it: three blanks, two marks, and a sixth space with a mark and nineteen columns of figures ruled long afterwards in a different hand with a nine taken out of the last one in a third hand, and the seventh line bare. The man of about thirty-four with a sheet keeps the only record in nine counties of what the page did, on his own sheet with no head over it, and it goes down the lane in his coat, which is the third time a number has gone down that lane in a coat in about six weeks. The register takes its eighth entry, the question a woman of about fifty asked in the first week of a month about being told before a page about her road is read at a gate, which the badge-man answered by saying there is no way to agree it, and no finding goes under it. And then the copyist says out loud that she wrote the nine into the last column of a slate in an apron on a Tuesday, that she did not know which way the woman's count ran either, that the end is where a figure you do not understand goes, and that she had not told her in about six weeks and is telling her now because the count has been read out loud by a man with a page. Nobody thanked her. The man with the sheet asks whether anything on the page is wrong, and Aren Kest says yes and will not say what and gives the reason: if he says it in that room then the next man to come up that stair with a page and a name at the top of it will be able to say that a man with a limp has told this room that a road is saved by somebody saying a number, and that is a page and he would have made it. He names the three people who acted without being sent and says he did not send them and will not claim them, and goes down. She comes out twenty minutes later. They pass. Nothing between them was described and no finding is under the eighth question.

## Volume 13, Batch 0003 — Chapters 621 to 630, "The Road He Closed"

**621 — Hearthmonth the twentieth, a Friday and not a market morning, the second hour of the night four days off, a set of works under an abbey that will take seven, and the third of six going cold on a night, 2,652 words, column 209 pence.** The works is three things: a course of stone across a channel, an oak beam with an iron strap, and a length of channel cut by somebody dead whose name is on nothing. It will take seven, it has taken seven since before anybody in that market town was in the room, seven roads go through it, and there were seven on it. Aren Kest is there because he went on the eighteenth to look at a bed and nobody sent him and nobody was waiting. At about the second hour the water comes over the road instead of under it and it is not weather. He looks at the beam first because that is what a man looks at, and the beam is sound, and then he finds a scour at the joint about a foot and a half across and a foot and a half deep with clean lime above and below, and says out loud that a course does not leave between two clean joints on its own, and then finds a second and newer scour at the turn of the channel with broken stone of a cut face in it, and says somebody has been in there with a bar and a wedge within a month, and says he does not know who and is not going to say why, and gives the reason on the page: a man who does not know should keep his mouth shut about a thing that can be used against somebody. The keeper of the third road, who has a number and a name and nineteen years of the job, put the water off it with a bar, a bucket, a boy of about fourteen and a dog, and said first of all that it was not him. The chapter then does the arithmetic in about two minutes at the second hour of the night with a hand lantern, and the finding is the volume's reversal in miniature: the gap did not go first, the gap sat on the works for nothing, and the marked roads paid for it. He had said the order wrong and the number right. A man who had come through in the autumn and said seven and given no name had been thinking about it ever since and had made the two sevens be the same seven, and was wrong.

**622 — Hearthmonth the twenty-first, a Saturday and a market morning, the second hour in the room over the weigh-house, a keeper who has walked four days with his boots in a bag, and a man of about forty-seven who is right and is not thanked, 2,918 words, column 221 pence.** The keeper of the third road comes up at the first hour and says the one thing that matters, which is that he has a number and a name against it and his name against the number and it is his job and he can be written to and will answer, and says it so the room will not spend a morning deciding whether he is a person. The copyist says out loud that that is the whole of what is different about him and that she has been waiting about four years to hear it. The badge-man says the sentence the room exists to hold: in nine counties there is exactly one road on that page anybody could write to tonight, and it is the one with a number on it, and every other road on it would have to be described by a person, and none of them has somebody standing on it who is willing to be described. The man of about forty-seven arrives, says he heard at a gate about nine miles back and has been sent nothing and sent nobody, and says the rest of it: six are marked to be held, there are seven on the works, one of the six went on Friday night, and the only line on the page with nothing against it is the seventh. A man near the back says take it off. Slade says he asked for six marks and got none and did not expect any, and then says the thing that makes the morning cost something: **the gap did not go first, a gap sat on the works for nothing and the roads with figures on them paid for it, and that is not a gap going first.** Nobody thanked him. A man of about thirty-one from the road office comes up with a schedule nobody asked for and says his office is going to send men and materials to the third and take the money off the works, and that he has been told to say so where it is being decided and to say that he has no number. The copyist will not name the gap. The woman of about twenty-nine offers to countersign a letter with nothing at the top of it, and the badge-man calls that the first piece of paper out of that room in four and a half years that anybody in nine counties had asked for, and says giving it cost nothing because the room had nothing in it to give. Aren Kest says the bed of the crossing and refuses to say who, and when asked what the seventh is going to be he says he does not know and asks that it be recorded as a thing said in front of nine people so that he is not asked twice and does not answer twice. Nobody wrote that down either.

**623 — Hearthmonth the twenty-sixth, a Thursday and a market morning, four days off, a new sill in three courses, a cart of stone, a boy of about seventeen, and a fourth course that is not laid, 2,260 words, column 233 pence.** The recovery beat, and it is the same paper at a small scale for the third time. Four men, a boy and a cart take out what is left of the old sill across the scoured reach and put three new courses in lime in about two hours of daylight. The man of about fifty who lays stone will not let anybody else lay a course and does not explain why, mixes the lime in the open air and says out loud that a man who mixes it in a heap goes off in the middle and sets like a stone and that in a year somebody will say the bed was laid bad and it will be true and it will not have been anybody's fault, **and that is worse than a fault.** The middle course goes in perfectly and he sits down on the parapet and will not lay the fourth, and gives the reason to about four men at a crossing and to the protagonist last and least: the bottom is bedded on the channel, the top is bedded on the one under it and carries the water every hour, and the one in between has nothing on it that will fall and nothing under it that will hold it. It is a good course, it is the best laid of the three, and it is the one that will go, and nobody will ever ask him about a middle course, because a bottom course is the ground and a top course is the water and the one in between has to be its own argument and has not got one. He says he would rather be a man who lays three and is never asked about it than a man who lays four. Aren Kest holds the string for three hours and says nothing at all, and the chapter is exact about why: a new sill is not work for a man with a bad leg on ground wet to the ankle, and everybody at that crossing knew it including him, and the boy of about seventeen was told to stop looking at the man with the stick as though he were going to be asked to do something. The wages go into five hands on a sound stone. The man of about fifty writes nothing down and the reason is not that a man with a sheet was four days back; it is that a keeper's day is not a return, and there is no return in nine counties in which a man may write down how many stones he laid, and he has asked three times and been refused twice for want of a head and once for want of a rule. He tells the boy that they will not have it and that they are right not to have it and that it will be a hole in about thirty years and he will not be here, and that if a man comes in thirty years with a bar and looks at the middle he will find three courses in lime and a clean bed and nothing wrong, and that is the best thing he has ever done and nobody will ever know and that is the correct arrangement and he is not going to be asked about it again.

**624 — Hearthmonth the twenty-eighth, a Saturday and a market morning, the second hour at the counter under the weigh-house, a woman of about forty who has come four days of carting with a load of lime and has not been asked anything, and a question asked a second time, 2,456 words, column 241 pence.** Nell Craddock comes up the lane behind a cart of quicklime with two of her own hands on the shafts and nobody sent her, and gives the whole of her reason to about four people before anybody asks her anything. She says out loud in the lane that a page has been read at her end twice and that she wants to know whether the seventh line is going to be shut. The badge-man gives the answer he gives and she says I did not ask you. She says she has not been asked anything by anybody in nine counties and is not complaining because she would not answer it, and that she heard a man say on Tuesday that the third road had water on it and that she heard it and did not go and look and is not going to anybody to be told. He is in the lane at the low end of the boards. She asks **Do you want it** and he does not answer, and she does not repeat it, and the chapter says on the page that she was not asking twice in the way people ask twice. She then tells him what she has, unasked, and says the first time anybody has said to her that she does not have to, and that it changes nothing: nine crossings open and the weeks the road is shut, held by a boy of about eleven who will be a man of about twenty and will not remember it as a count, and she has not said how long any shut week runs and it has never once been a number of days in anybody's mouth. The man of about thirty-four with a sheet points out she has just said it to him, and she says she noticed a month ago that he is the only person she has met who keeps something with no head over it, and thinks about it a great deal, and it does not make her give it, because a man who keeps a sheet in a satchel is a man who will hand it up, and it does make her notice, and those are different and the second is worth a walk. He did not write it down. Slade comes through, and she says she hopes his road is all right, and that she heard the third had gone cold and that she was sorry about the third, and he says the third has a keeper and a number, and that was all. The hearing stands in the street. At the fifth hour he goes up the lane towards the road office and turns off at the first turning, because there is a hired room over a saddler's in that lane which is not the weigh-house room and is shut on no day, and about four people saw him turn and about five did not.

**625 — Hearthmonth the twenty-ninth, a Sunday and not a market morning, the second hour of the night in a hired room over a saddler's, about nine people, nine minutes, and a decision nobody conferred, 3,539 words, column 253 pence.** The centre of the block, and the longest chapter in it. The nine are named and counted. He puts it on the table in about nine minutes and the chapter says the room is about nine people and about four of them have said since that they could have stopped it and had nothing to stop it with, and about five have said they did not understand what they were watching. He names the three things he has and none of them is a figure. The woman of about thirty-four who keeps the key asks **On whose authority** and he says **Mine** and says he has no badge and no office and tells them which of the two things it is: it is a man talking in a room. The copyist says it is a man talking in a room and that she is not going to write it down and there is nowhere in that room to put it, and that she is the only person in the room who has spent four and a half years making sure a sentence in a mouth does not become a line in a book, and he has just said a sentence in a mouth. Slade asks for the consequence and not the decision, and is told six roads carry what six roads carry, and then makes him say the seventh out loud in a way nobody can write down differently, and the seventh is the small road and it is a road on the days when two people turn up at the two ends of it and there are two people and one of them is about sixty. The man of about thirty-four asks the only question anybody can act on: **Who tells her?** and he does not know. The man of about thirty-one then offers the machinery — a schedule, a column, a heading, a number at the top of it, four hours of riding and a clerk who does not know her name — and is told do not do that, and the reason is that a page that tells a woman about her own road is a page that tells a man at the top of it what a road is worth, and I am not going to build a second one in a room at the second hour of the night on a Sunday. The copyist then says the thing the chapter was built to reach: the room's standing that a road may be described only by its own answer is **the reason there is no list of keepers in nine counties and therefore no address for anybody**, and nobody in the room disagreed with the reason and nobody was willing to break the standing to get a rider out of it in nine minutes. The badge-man says that nobody in that room had the power to stop it and every one of them had the standing to try, that those are two different things, that a thing said there is a man talking and not a direction, a rule, a finding, or anything in any book, that on Monday the six will carry what six carry and the seventh nothing, that there is not one thing that happened that anybody can be blamed for, and that in about four months somebody is going to be praised for it. Then he says I have not asked to do this, and that nobody is going to get the chance tonight, and gives his reason: he would rather be wrong tonight than be able to say that in a year six went. Slade says that is my sentence. Nobody is thanked. The man of about thirty-four writes the whole of it in words on his own sheet with no head over it, puts no number on it, gives his reason for not doing so, and records that it was the fourth thing down that lane in a coat in about six weeks and that three of the four were numbers and this one was not and was the only one anybody had ever had to act on.

**626 — Hearthmonth the thirtieth, a Monday and not a market morning, the third hour at the counter, a man of about thirty-four with a sheet and nowhere to put a name, and the finding that there was never an arrangement, 2,560 words, column 268 pence.** He wants to put a name on it and does not have one, and tells the lane what a clerk in a district office said: a line is not a road, and a number on a piece of paper is not a thing I can send a rider to. He asks the copyist what the address is and she says there is not one, and then says the whole of it, which she has never said out loud in four and a half years: **there is no address, not for that road and not for any of the seven, because a list of keepers is a list of roads and a road may not be described here until it has answered, and that is the correct standing, and it is why two contradictory warnings about four miles of channel sat in two books for three weeks, and it is why the two in three of about nine hundred drying cloths was never counted, and it is why a man of about thirty-four with a sheet cannot write a name about a woman four days of carting away and will not be able to tomorrow and there is nobody he can go and be angry at.** The badge-man says he has spent thirty-one years saying to rooms that there is no head in nine counties and has never once had to say it about a woman, and is not going to pretend it is a comfort. The man of about thirty-four says he has carried a thing with a name on it or a gate to go to for four and a half years and has never had to decide who to write to, only when to be late, and that he has been in four counties and asked about nine keepers and could put four of them on a piece of paper, and has never once thought about the other five until this month. The road office clerk comes down the lane, says before anybody speaks that he is not going to use the schedule, and puts the two facts side by side: the machinery was ready at seven in the morning and a man told it not to at two in the morning, about five hours apart, and neither of them knew. The keeper of the room says from the door not to put anything at the top of it, and the reason the second one is right is that the second one is right. Then the chapter turns to the absence: Aren Kest comes down the lane a quarter of a mile off at the fourth hour going towards the coast with a stick and a day's food, does not come to the counter, goes past it four hundred paces short on the other side without slowing down, and the reason is given in full — he could have gone four days and got there, and if he had gone he would have arrived on the fourth day instead of in a market, and there is no arrangement in nine counties by which a man four days off is fetched for anything, and he is not a keeper of that road and not a man with a badge and not a man anybody has to answer to, and if he had stopped he would have had to say the sentence out loud, and then there would have been a person to be angry with, and she has spent two months making sure a road with no figure on it is not a road anybody can do a favour to or a favour against. **He did not want to be the person on that road. He wanted the road to have no person on it.**

**627 — Goatmonth the first, a Tuesday, the first of a month and not a market morning, the second hour at the counter, a man of about forty-seven who came on foot because he said he would, 2,263 words, column 279 pence.** The badge-man says the hearing stands in the lane and not in a room, and says why: there is no there this morning, and said in a room it would sound like a thing a room does. Slade comes on foot, keeps the date he kept, and says he has not come to argue. The man of about thirty-four reports that the works has six on it, that six is what it will take, that the six marked to be held are holding, that the third has not been wet since the twenty-second, and that a carter told him so and did not want to be involved. Slade says then my arithmetic is paid, and says what has been paid and what has not: six roads are open that would not have been open, and a man is being paid in a way he did not expect and he has noticed and is not going to enjoy it. The copyist asks whether he has a figure for the seventh and he says no, and says he has begun to understand that not having one is not the same as having nothing, and that this is a thing he has got from the room and not from the arithmetic. The man of about thirty-four sets out what a closure actually is: on Sunday night, in a room over a saddler's, in about nine minutes, on the word of a man with a limp, and there is no sheet for it, no head over it, not in any book, and not in a district schedule because a road office clerk was told at two in the morning not to use one. Slade says then he has got the answer to the only question he has been asking anybody for two months, and it came in a room, and nobody in nine counties can hand it to anybody, and in four months he will have to say a man told him in a room, and a man with a story. The badge-man says that is the correct position and the whole of the block, and that it is worth exactly nothing, and that the whole of the difference between this morning and the ninth of a month is that a man has now done a thing instead of a rule having been found correct. About four of the nine say a rule with a head would have stopped it and about five say a rule with a head would have made it legal, and the two sets do not overlap. The copyist then says the part that was new: the page is going round a second time, a blank in a second printing is not evidence of anything, and this is the first time in about two months she has wanted something to be argued with and has not got a place to put it. The man of about thirty-four says the ninth thing he has been writing down for two months, which is not about roads: in about four and a half years nothing in that room has ever depended on anybody being in it except three mornings, two of them the mornings a page came into the county and the third Sunday night in a room over a saddler's, and in every one of the three the man wanted was a man with a limp, and he was never sent, and nobody has ever asked him whether he would come. The badge-man says it is true and that it is the second time in a month he has heard it and that he has no answer and that a man with no answer should say so rather than produce one. Aren Kest stands in the lane four hundred paces short of the boards for about an hour and nobody speaks to him.

**628 — Goatmonth the third, a Thursday and a market morning, the second hour in the market street, a woman of about forty who has been four days of carting and has not been asked anything, and a man from a road office buying a basket, 2,582 words, column 296 pence.** She is in the market before the room is fully open, with an empty cart and a debt to collect. A man of about thirty-one from the road office says it to the middle distance while putting down a basket: four days, took a road off the works under the abbey on Sunday night, seventh line, nobody had to tell me, I read it in a schedule that came back with a blank on it, and there was a note that said the road had been taken off on a man's word in a room, which is not a thing a schedule has a space for. She asks him for the name of the road. He does not have one, because the schedule carries a line and a number and a district and not names. She says I keep the seventh line. He says he is sorry and did not know and that there is nobody in the office he could have told and he did not know there was anybody to tell. She says that is the whole of what she came to say and she has been saying it to herself on a slope for four days. Then she says it in a street, in a working voice, to about nine people: **nobody told me, four days of carting and not one person came up the slope, nobody left anything at the kiln, nobody said anything at the top end to the woman who has held that end for about thirty-one years, and I have been up there twice since Monday and both times she asked me whether I had heard anything and both times I said no, and I had not been told a thing and I said no on the strength of a woman of about sixty asking me twice, and I have not slept properly about it since.** And then: **nobody sent anybody**, from the man of about thirty-four with a sheet, who says he has carried a sheet with a line on it for four days and has been to a district office and was told not to use the schedule and did not, and that he would not have known how to deliver it if he had, because a line is not a road and a road here has no keeper against its name, and that this is not a thing he did to her, it is a thing that was in front of him and he did not do anything about it, and she is entitled to that sentence and he is not entitled to a better one. The badge-man says there was no arrangement, that he has never in thirty-one years had to tell a woman that nobody was going to tell her, that he has looked for the arrangement and there is not one, and that there is not a bad arrangement, there is not any. The copyist says there is a reason and it is hers and it is the standing and she is not taking it back, and the woman of about forty says then it is you, and the copyist says it is the standing and not me, and asks to be allowed to be correct about it without it being a comfort to either of them. Then the only question she came to ask: **Is the count to be kept?** and the man of about thirty-four says keep it, and says there is nowhere to put it and that is not a reason to stop, and that he has thought about one of the five for the first time and cannot do anything about it, and that she should keep it and the boy should go on having it and neither of those is his to tell her. She will tell the woman at the top end on Friday and she will not be pleased and she will not say so, and the two of them have never once asked anybody to do anything for them. And then the sentence the block is built on, to the nearest person who has spoken to her: you have all been very careful, and there was nobody in nine counties who could have been uncareful, and that is not the same as anybody being careless and it is not a defence either and I am not offering anybody one; I am not saying you were cruel, I am saying it happened, it happened on a Sunday night in a room and it happened to a road and it happened to a boy of about eleven who has a count in his head that nobody has ever written down, and I have not got a word for the other thing and I have been looking for one since Monday and I have not found it and I am not going to make one up, and I would like it understood that I have not found one because there is not one and not because I am slow. Nobody thanked her and nobody told her she was right. Aren Kest is about forty paces off and has been since the second hour and she has known the whole time and has not come over.

**629 — Goatmonth the twenty-fourth, a Thursday and a market morning, the second hour in the room, a woman of about forty-three who has got her count and has come four days to give it to nobody, and the finding that nobody being hurt is not a figure anybody can use, 2,804 words, column 318 pence.** Twenty-five days after, which is three weeks and four days. The man of about thirty-four asks whether anybody in the room has been on the seventh line since the twenty-ninth of a month and nobody has, and then makes the mistake: he says nobody was hurt, nothing went late, nothing rotted, no wages went anywhere, and he is not going to call that a bill. The copyist refuses it in front of nine people and gives the reason, which is the room's own rule turned against itself: **that is not a good report, that is the absence of a report, and if a road produces no returns then a road can be shut for four years and nothing arrives and a road doing perfectly well produces nothing either, and the two of them look identical in nine counties, and every single thing in this room is built so that a thing that looks identical in nine counties does not get set beside another thing, and the rule has just cost a road twenty-five days of anybody knowing anything and will go on costing.** The road office clerk has read a blank column as good news and is told he cannot know that, and that in about four years somebody will shut a road that produces no returns on a blank in a schedule and put it in a report as a thing that was tried and did not cost anything, and the road will not be there and the report will be true. The badge-man asks what the cost is and answers it himself: **nobody being hurt is the answer a man gives when he has not found the cost; it is not a finding, it is a gap with a comfortable shape in it.** And the copyist says the only cost she found in twenty-five days is that it cost the woman of about forty the only thing she has, which is not being asked, and that she has been looking for a better one because a better one would have gone in a column. Then Hester Vane comes up the outside stair after four days on foot and says before anything else that her line is the fifth and that nobody was to make anything of her being here. Her road stood aside on the eighteenth at the gate, she said the count was to be taken first by the people who walk it, on the ground, and the badge-man could not grant it and was not being asked to, and the road's own people have taken it. Asked to say the count, she says no, and says that is the whole of what she came to say, and that she is going to say it once and then sit down, because they are going to ask three times and she is going to say no three times and they can have that in the room instead of in the lane. She says it is ours, and it is the crossings that are open and the weeks the road is shut, and that has never once been a number of days in anybody's mouth and is not going to start now in this room, in a drawer, on a schedule or in a column. She came to say that it was taken and that nothing came of it, and says she is aware that is the least interesting sentence anybody has said in that room in four and a half years and has said it anyway, because in about four months somebody is going to shut a road and point at the eighteenth and say it is a thing that has been done before. The woman of about fifty, in from the back, says in four seconds that it is not a thing that has been done before, and Hester Vane says she is not asking anybody to cite hers, and then says to the room and not to the two of them that nobody is to say her road was shut, because it was not, it stood aside, and those are not the same and a room is the second worst place in nine counties to say it and a gate is the first. Nobody said the two roads are the same road and nobody asked. Then the badge-man sets out the three: the culvert at the gate a month past, where a man with a limp was the only person who could see a middle course that had gone and said so twice to two people and neither was the road office; the crossing under an abbey on the twentieth, where he was the only person there at the second hour of the night; and the room over a saddler's, where this time he acted on it. Those are the three, and in the first two the man was right, nobody was harmed, and nothing came of it at all. The man of about thirty-four writes three in words and says why he did not put it in a column: it is not a measurement, and three in a column beside something else would have given the room a number to use by Friday.

**630 — Thawmonth the first, YR 321, a Thursday and a market morning and the first of a month, the second hour in the room, a register that has stood at eight entries for about a month, and the answer a man gives to a question nobody asked, 2,810 words, column 341 pence.** The year turned on the first of a month and nobody in the lane turned with it, and the first thing written with the new year against it was a date in the corner of a roll book in the same hand. The hearing stands. Aren Kest is not in the room; he is in the lane, and about four people saw him and about five did not, and nobody went and told him there was a stair open. The page has been round a second time and stands where it stood: three with a mark and three with nothing in them, of which two are refusals and one has not been reached and the page cannot tell. The man of about thirty-four says what the third time round will be: a fourth blank, the first space on the sheet, a man who has read it and would not, and nothing whatever on the page to say so, and he has a piece of paper with no head over it that says which one it was, and nobody asks him to prove it. The copyist asks what goes in, the keeper of the room says say it and do not soften it, and the ninth entry is the question whether a room with a door on a lane and a number and a deed of thirteen words may hold a thing decided in a room with no head over it, and if not, where that thing goes. She answers both halves: it may not; the answer to the second is a drawer about eleven inches deep, and it is not a place, it is a place so that a thing is not lost, and a room can hold a question and a thing somebody has refused to sign, and what it cannot hold is a decision, because a decision that lands there has nowhere to be challenged, and a decision that cannot be challenged is not a decision but a thing that happened to nine people in a cold room at the second hour of a Sunday night. It goes in as a question and stays a question and no finding goes under it, and she would like the man who wrote it and the man who decided it both to be in the room when somebody tries. The badge-man says he will be. The man of about thirty-four says his arithmetic is paid and that this is the first of the four times the room has found a reason to let him do nothing, and that in about four months the same arithmetic runs again and there will be no line with nothing against it, and the thing that goes first will be a road with a number and a name and a keeper, and he has not got a name for which one and will not guess, because a name would be at the top of the next page before it went out and he will not hand nine counties a list. Then the keeper of the room says the last thing in that room, about a road and not about anybody in it: the year turned this morning, she has watched two years turn and neither changed anything the room holds, the seven is still seven, the page is still going round, the register is at nine questions, the fourth head is still ruled and empty, the press still stands open with no date, a leaf lies face down in a room over a saddler's that nobody has turned over and that nobody in that room is minded to turn over, and a road four days of carting from the counter is still being carried on the days when two people turn up at the two ends of it, and no date on any paper in that room makes that road better or worse. At the seventh hour the room shuts and the key goes down and the woman of about twenty-nine comes out about twenty minutes later and goes past him and neither of them says anything, and that is the whole of what happened between them in a month. And then, in the lane, to about four people who can hear and about five who cannot, and nobody having asked: **I wanted it.** He says nobody has asked him that in two volumes and about a hundred and thirty days, that he did not say anything when a woman of about forty asked him at a counter, that he is saying it now because nobody is going to ask, and that it is not a defence and he would rather be a man who wanted a thing and knew that about nine households on a road are carrying what six were carrying than a man who was asked and said no and did it anyway. The copyist says from the doorway that it is not in the register, and he says he would not want it to be, and says why: it would be a finding about a man and the register is for questions, and a man asked afterwards and answering afterwards has not been asked; a thing I say in a lane on a Thursday to four people who can hear me is not being asked, it is the other thing, and I have wanted it and I have said so and it is in no book in nine counties and it does not make anything true, and the sixth road will hold until it does not, and I will be asked again in about four months and there will be no head in nine counties then either. Nobody thanked him. The year went on being a new one. He goes out on the made road towards the coast at the eighth hour, nobody had told him anything, he had asked nobody for anything, and there was nothing in the lane behind him that would ever be written down.

**631 — Thawmonth the third, YR 321, a Saturday and a market morning, the second hour in the market street and the third hour in a room over a weigh-house, nine people who had not met before, 2,573 words, column 246 pence.** The man of about fifty-eight with a badge has no way to call a meeting and says so out loud in the market street to nobody in particular, and puts no name at the top of anything, and about nine people hear him and about four care, and one of the four is a man of about fifty-two who keeps a road. The copyist asks why a street and he answers that a head is the only thing in nine counties that can make a person come and he has not got one, and that he has got a lane about four hundred paces long and a market that finishes at the ninth hour. The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room says from the door that it is a records room and that it is shut on every day but a market morning and she will not have it opened for nine people on a Tuesday, and that the register is at nine questions and the ninth has been standing unanswered for about a month and she is not going to be handed a second one. The copyist says she will keep the three series, will not merge them, will not write a rule in a book of questions, and will read out with the door open anything written and sent to this room and it will go nowhere else. The man of about fifty-two arrives and will not say which road he keeps and gives the reason, that the moment one of them has the name of it they have a road with a name against it and everything that happens to it gets argued about on paper instead of on the ground, and he would rather be a person in a room than a name on a list. The man of about forty-seven calls that the best argument anybody has made in that room in about four and a half years. A man of about fifty-one from the glass country and a woman of about thirty-six from the burned-list county come. Two letters arrive in the band and the copyist puts them face up and does not open them and says the room has asked nothing. The woman of about fifty says the three clauses out loud once and says she is not saying it is right. Nobody thanked anybody. Aren Kest was at the low end of the counter boards for about an hour and went up no stair.

**632 — Thawmonth the sixth, YR 321, a Tuesday and not a market morning, the fourth hour at the counter boards with the room shut behind them, 2,595 words, column 272 pence.** The room is shut and the keeper of the room says on the page that it is shut on every day but a market morning and she will not have it opened, and that on a Saturday she will put the door open and read out whatever has been written. About nine people stand on wet boards with no market. The badge-man reads the three sentences off the back of an unsigned sheet twice and rules that nobody is going to say what happened on the night of the twenty-ninth of a month, and gives the reason, that a group which goes back over what it did has already decided what it is for. The man of about forty-seven takes the sentences apart, says notice takes time and there is no time, says in about four months the same arithmetic runs again with no gap and something with a number and a name and a keeper has to come off and the notice clause will be the reason it comes off about nine days late, and says I am right, and says he would rather have a rule that is wrong than no rule, and says he has not changed his mind. The badge-man agrees with him and says the point is not that it binds anybody. The man of about fifty-two asks what the point is and the badge-man says in about four months there will be about nine people who have already said out loud that a thing like that may not happen without notice and a witness, and that is worth exactly what a thing that cannot be obeyed is worth and it is not nothing. The man of about thirty-four with a sheet then says the notice is not for the road, it is for the man proposing to shut it, and rewrites the clause so that it is a clause about a man and not about a road, and says it will be easier to break because breaking it does not require anybody to be cruel, only a man in a hurry. The man of about fifty-one asks the man of about forty-seven whether he has got a name in his head and is told he has got a shape and not a road and will not convert it, because a name in a head is not a name on a page. The woman of about fifty says one sentence about the day it happens and does not make it into a rule. The man of about thirty-one from a road office describes his own office. Hester Vane comes up the lane four days walked and says her name out loud in the open before she says anything else and says nobody is to write down that her road did anything first. Nobody thanked anybody.

**633 — Thawmonth the tenth, YR 321, a Saturday and a market morning, the second hour with the door open, two pieces of paper read out loud, 2,638 words, column 291 pence.** The two letters went up the outside stair in the middle of a week's returns and the copyist put them face up and did not open them for half a minute, and then read them out. The coast of returning roads writes that it will not send anybody because the only person who could be spared is the one who holds the post and a post does not get left, that anything sent to the post will be read and not answered, and that it would rather say so in its own hand than be asked in a month for an answer it could not give. The second letter is longer, is in the hand of a woman of about sixty who has held the far end of a road for about thirty-one years, came down off a slope on the back of a cart with a sale on it carried by the woman of about forty who runs a lime kiln, who did not come up the stair and asked for nothing. It says that nobody in nine counties had ever asked that road a question it could answer, that every question that had come near it had been a question about a number, that the first number anybody says out loud about a place is the number that place has for the rest of its life and is said by whoever says it first, that about four houses owe her for a season and about five do not, and that anybody who wants the count is welcome to come and walk it and it understood there was no time. The room is quiet. The man of about fifty-two turns his chair round, says he came on Saturday and would not say a road and has been proud of it and has just been handed a road with more nerve about its own name than he has about his, and turns back in about a minute. The copyist says a road's own words go nowhere, that is why they cannot be got at and turned into a line, and that it is worth nothing this morning and she is not taking it back. Hester Vane says do not use that letter, that a notice is a thing somebody carries and a clause cannot make a person at the top of a slope put a letter on a loaded cart, and that the argument against the rule is in the letter. The man of about forty-seven says write it anyway. The woman of about fifty comes down to the boards and asks for the words that go in it, and the notice clause is written and the man of about thirty-four's last four words are about a day the road is being used. Berta Lomax reads it out twice and says the word end is doing more work in that sentence than any other word and that she does not know what it means on a road with nine crossings open. Nobody answered her.

**634 — Thawmonth the fifteenth, YR 321, a Thursday and a market morning, the second hour in the room, nine days given out loud, 2,268 words, column 304 pence.** The badge-man gives them nine days and gives the reason, that the longest thing anybody in nine counties ever agreed to was about nine days and it did not hold. He is asked what it was and says no, and says he is not going to tell that story in a room that is about to write three clauses, and nobody asked him again in a month, which is the only thing anybody asked for in this block and did not get. Hester Vane argues the witness clause for four days and says a witness is not a signature and that a signature is a thing you can write. The man of about forty-seven puts the whole objection to it out loud before she does, that a witness who can be named and found is a person and a person can be put out of the way and this room cannot protect one and every keeper in nine counties will choose the road. The room writes it anyway and the last five words, that if the witness cannot be found afterwards the road is open, are hers, and the man of about thirty-one wants them in because they will cost his office the most. The woman of about thirty-six asks whether a road can refuse to name a witness, answers it out loud, that then nothing happens and that is what refusing means and the clause works because a road was allowed to say no, and says there is not one word in either clause about the right to say no and that is the part somebody ought to read out loud one day in a room with about nine people in it, and asks whether anybody thinks that is dangerous, and nobody answers, and the badge-man refuses to have it answered early. The man of about fifty-two says he was holding out against being anybody's witness.
