**`chapter-0677.md`, 3,204 words.** Embermonth the thirteenth, a Tuesday, not a market morning, the room shut, about nine people at the boards in a street that is not a market. **The cost of the block is a person.** About nine tons of cut stone stand in the low approach to a crossing nine miles off because a Crown crew put them there on the fourth of Fallowmonth and did not come back, and four of about nine households at that crossing go over a bank in nine minutes a day and have said no to nothing. **A woman of about forty-four who keeps that crossing walks nine miles to say no to a paper before she is asked it.** She says she has already said no to one Crown paper once. **Her reason is a crossing, and a Tuesday, and the man who keeps that crossing with her being told no by a man in a livery who was polite about it and wrote it down and read it back.** She is not a villain and she does not shout. The badge-man says in nine words that a thing that cannot take orders cannot sign anything and that it is not a hole. The man of about thirty-four works out out loud that nine counties has written a plan that cannot be signed. The two of them do not speak again in this block. Chapter body 2,954.

**`chapter-0678.md`, 3,035 words.** Embermonth the sixteenth, a Friday, not a market morning, a lane in a county two days off at about the ninth hour in the rain, nine households on it. **The smallest possible version of a thing, and it costs somebody.** The question a woman of about thirty-nine walked two days in the wet in Rainmonth to ask, and that the copyist could not answer, is on the page. A man of about thirty-one puts a board on the inside of nine gates in about four minutes each, reading back what he wrote, and at the ninth gate **the woman of about thirty-nine comes out of her own gate four feet before he reaches it and says no before he asks.** He says there is no line on his page for a person who has said no to a question nobody asked, and he puts it on the back of the page for the ninth gate under the board where a person can find it. **She says she cannot promise to say it twice and can promise that about nine people on that lane will know somebody said it first.** Nobody thanked anybody. Chapter body 2,748.

**`chapter-0679.md`, 3,230 words.** Embermonth the seventeenth, a Saturday, a market morning. **Two roads write and two roads send nothing, and the two that send nothing are not the two the column is standing on, and a woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a horse says that difference out loud.** Nine households are counted at the boards for four roads walked to: eighteen, eleven, eleven, seven, four, four, four and nine, and the man of about thirty-four says his own house gives nothing before anybody reads it out and is not argued with, and the yard opposite is argued about and then not, and it comes to seventy pence on the back of a sheet. **The second cost of this volume is said out loud again in a different mouth: a road that has decided something is not a return and is not a roll and is not a sheet going out to a district, and the three series cannot hold the only thing that has happened.** Then the ordinary half: nine people turn out at about the fourth hour at a barrel drain nobody ordered, a hundred and forty barrow trips, and a man of about fifty-one who has been standing in nine inches of water on Fridays for nine weeks and says so and is told he will not be thanked for it and will not be argued with, which is the arrangement. Chapter body 2,921.

**`chapter-0680.md`, 3,481 words.** Wolfmonth the first, a Saturday, a market morning, the first of a month. **The block's close.** Nine hundred words stand on a table under a cloth in a room over a saddler's with nobody's name on them and nobody's name taken off them, and two people have tried to take a copy and been refused and neither was argued with. **The copyist does not read the letter and says in nine words that it stands and that it is a decision and not a promise broken.** The badge-man says the standing defect paid in nine words and does not move the figure, and the answer to whether being right early was a kindness stands where it was given. **The plan is not withdrawn, and the reason is that the fifth, sixth and seventh clauses are the same three about what goes in a book and what a person is called, and the sixth is not going to be changed because the only person who has checked the nine hundred words does not live here.** **And the small concrete local decision is not the protagonist's: a woman of about fifty-nine who keeps the second gate from the top of the mile and a half, who gave the horse and has never been asked for anything in about thirty-four years and does not know what to do with a person who asks, decides to go and ask somebody one question, and nobody sent her.** The chapter's last act is a knock on a door at the top of that lane in the rain. Nobody thanked anybody. Chapter body 3,197.

---

## Volume 14, Batch 0004 — Chapters 681 to 690, "The Coin That Was Not Offered"

**`chapter-0681.md`, 3,805 words.** Wolfmonth the eighth, a Saturday, a market morning, the second hour, the hearing standing in the street, about four hundred people. **The block's promise: a coalition that exists without a document.** A woman of about twenty-four who keeps a copy-book and the front of her shop has copied the nine hundred words three times for nobody and asks out loud what the thing is called, so that she can write a word at the top of a book. **The copyist gives the three numbers in the flat way: about four people in nine counties have been asked things this year, about nine people have come ready to say no, and about four of the nine who came are the only ones who could carry anything, meaning a person who will walk four days up a made road and stand in front of a person and say one question and come back.** The badge-man says the load-bearing sentence of the block, in eighteen words, that a thing with no head is not a smaller thing, it is a harder thing to get at, and then refuses to call the thing anything, because naming a thing is how a thing gets got at. **Aren Kest is four hundred paces off at the weigh-house and the copyist says so out loud at the boards and refuses in advance to say why.** Four carriers are asked in the open, one at a time, and all four say no in about four seconds, and the reason that stands is that a person who carries a thing for nine counties is a head. **The small close is the copy-book carried back to a shop front, shut, and put on a shelf on its side against the dust, with nine hundred words set out on a board in her own hand and no word at the top of it.** Nobody thanked anybody and nothing was decided. Chapter body 3,548.

**`chapter-0682.md`, 3,760 words.** Wolfmonth the thirteenth, a Thursday, a market morning, the second hour, the boards. **The second mouth of the roads, and the difference from the last block.** The man of about thirty-four with a satchel has been out on his own account and asked four roads a different question, and two wrote and two sent nothing. **And this time the two that sent nothing are the two the column is standing on, the coast road and the four miles at the ford, and the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a horse says that difference out loud and says the shape has turned over, and that the only people who could say a word about a road read off a list are the two who are not writing.** A road that has written is not a return, is not a roll, and is not a sheet going out to a district, and the third series cannot carry the answer, said by the copyist in a market street in a fourth form, and not resolved. **The badge-man says one thing, twelve words, and it is the sentence from Chapter 0681 in a shorter form.** A man of about forty-four who sells nails asks then why write to them at all, and the answer is that a road that has written once will write again, and a road that writes twice has decided something, and that if he stops writing the only answers this county has are the ones a man in a livery gets at a gate. **Aren Kest says that he has not been up either of the two roads and is not going to, and that nobody in this county has stood on them, and that he did not arrange that.** A man of about thirty-eight who puts boards on gates is in the market and is not asked about the fourteenth of Rainmonth, because the second time a person is asked is the time they have to have an answer ready. The last act is the man of about thirty-four writing the day and the two roads on the back of a sheet and refusing to put it in a book. Nobody thanked anybody. Chapter body 3,474.

**`chapter-0683.md`, 3,595 words.** Wolfmonth the sixteenth, a Sunday, not a market morning, with the room over the weigh-house shut behind its own door. **The tenth question, ruled in nine hands of minutes.** The room is eleven paces by eight, the window has not been opened since the middle of Longlight, the press stands open and is not dated, the deed is thirteen words and costs fourpence a year, the register has nine questions and no finding under any, and the fourth ruled head over a place where a finding goes is empty and has been for about ten months, and the day-book has ten entries, and a drawer about eleven inches deep holds about nine leaves with no head over them and is not counted. **The copyist reads the nine questions aloud to an empty room, keeps a thumb on the empty head, says out loud that she is not filling it and is not ruling a fifth head, writes the tenth question on a leaf, writes the day in the day-book, and rules nothing.** The tenth question is, on the page: **of a thing with no head, and of a road that will not be named, what may be said to have been decided, and by whom it may be brought to.** The badge-man comes on foot on a day he has never come and objects that a question with no head over it will be read as a finding in about four years, **and is refused in sixteen words, that it will be a question because she wrote a question, and then she says out loud that a book does not say, and that a person who decides that about four and a half years of the county was a county that put down what it knew can live with it.** The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room says that the fourth head ought not to be there, is right, says so twice in about four and a half years, and is not argued with, and the copyist says no in about four seconds, and it is not hers to rule and not hers to take. **Aren Kest comes up the lane at about the fourth hour and does not knock on a shut room, and the copyist sees him through the window and does not get up, and the woman of about twenty-nine says nothing about it, and both doors stay shut.** Nobody thanked anybody. Chapter body 3,276.

**`chapter-0684.md`, 3,666 words.** Wolfmonth the twentieth, a Thursday, a market morning, the second hour, about four hundred people and about two hundred stopped. **One road refuses to name a witness.** A man of about fifty-two who keeps four miles of made road under contract for about nineteen years comes up the market street on foot with about nine men behind him at an agreed distance, and says he will not give the witness clause a name, and gives the reason, which is that a man who asks for four days is a man who finds out what he is when a thing is on a page. **And then he says the sentence the block is built on: the man with the badge put a rule in this county about four and a half ago about a road being shut, a shut road has to be proposed by somebody, a burden only falls on a man who proposed, that is a good rule and the best thing anybody in nine counties has ever made, and in four and a half years not one road has been shut, and the man who said a road might be shut has not shown that it may be shut, and he is not giving a man a name for a rule about a thing that has never happened to anybody.** Nobody in that market street softens it afterwards. **The badge-man makes it the sentence of the block by saying it twice, out loud, in a market street, in the man's own words, and saying why: in about nine days a serjeant is going to arrive with a sheet and a question, and about nine people in nine counties are going to be asked whether the second clause is a good rule or whether the man with the badge said a road might be shut, and he is not going to let anybody be asked that in a room.** The woman of about thirty-six says what the witness clause is for, which is the four and a half years in which a road got a name by somebody standing in a lane being wrong in public, and says she is not going to say whether the refusal is the best thing that has happened or the end of the only thing anybody built. **Aren Kest says the sixth clause is wrong, that he is not going to change it, and says it once more so that a man who has just refused to name nine men can hear that the man who wrote nine hundred words asking for a name on each side has not changed his mind.** The last act is nine men walking out of a market street at the fourth hour and the man of about fifty-two saying at the low end of the boards that his road is not a thing for anybody in that street to write down. Nobody thanked anybody. Chapter body 3,426.

**`chapter-0685.md`, 3,881 words.** Wolfmonth the twenty-seventh, a Thursday, a market morning. **The cost of asking, and it is a person.** A woman of about forty-nine who keeps a kitchen and four beds on the made road comes two miles and about nine hundred paces down off the made road to say in about nine minutes what two men in livery did at her door, once, and does not raise her voice. **A man of about thirty-one in a livery came on the Friday before last and asked her for a witness, and she said no, and he wrote it down, read it back, and said it was very good of her. On the Tuesday he came back and said he wanted a person who could read a number out loud, and she said the boy of about fourteen could not read, and he said he would find out, and he did, in her own yard, at about the fourth hour, and wrote it down, and read it back, and said it was very good of the boy.** The boy went on the Wednesday, the yard went with him, and a fortnight of a hired boy at fourpence a day and two beds of trade out of four are the price. She defends the man who took the boy, and says the word twice is the wrong word and that she has not got a better one, and says nobody has been hurt and nobody killed and nobody told anything, and refuses to make the boy into a thing. **The copyist asks whether he asked her twice for the same thing, and the answer is no, and that is the whole of what happened.** **A man of about thirty-four with a satchel then puts the shape of it in the street, that about four of the nine have been asked twice and every one of the second visits was for a thing the person had not been asked before, and she answers him in a hundred and three words: I was asked twice, I refused the first and I did not refuse the second, being refused is free, and being asked for something you have not been asked for is not free, and I am about forty-nine and I keep a kitchen.** The badge-man says the true thing in nine words, that the rule is not the difficulty, the asking is, and then does the arithmetic out loud in a market street on purpose and gets a woman's answer, that a kitchen is not a hearing and the only useful speakers in nine hearings are the ones who came on their own account. **A household of about five at the market town's own gate is asked to put its name to a list and says no, and the man who asked admits he had no right to ask.** Aren Kest says he has eaten in the kitchen twice and did not ask whether he was to be charged, and is told to pay her, and pays her at the boards in coins. The last act is a kitchen at the ninth hour with no boy of about fourteen at the end of a long table and a man of about thirty-three saying the boy is not to be asked for twice in a month. Nobody thanked anybody. Chapter body 3,652.

**`chapter-0686.md`, 3,852 words.** Frostmonth the first, a Monday, not a market morning, the first of a month, the room over the weigh-house shut behind its own door and the hearing not sitting because there is no market. **The recovery beat, and it is the volume's own longest stretch of ordinary labour.** The water has been coming over the crown of the made road at the market town's own gate since the eighth of Embermonth, and under the road is a culvert with three courses of sill and the middle course gone, and a hole under about nine feet of made road with no hole to put a hand in, and the stone for a fourth course lying in a line on the bank with the mallet on top of it where anybody coming in can see it. **Nine people turn out in the rain and nobody orders it: a mason of about fifty who lays stone at that gate and has for about twenty-six years, a crew of nine men off a yard under a contract taken out of an advance party, a man of about sixty-two with a wain and a team of four who comes with three, a woman of about thirty-eight with a bar and about nine buckets, and about nine households whose gates are inside the nine hundred yards of made road that drains into that cut.** The work is nine feet of silt and a chain on a bar; the turns arrange themselves in about nine minutes; the mason says you get your arms in from the side and not from the top. **The boy of about seventeen is on the bucket end from the fifth hour and counts in fives and loses his place twice.** A lamp is lit at the fourth hour because the light goes at about half past three in Frostmonth, and a man of about thirty with a bar holds it over the cut for about an hour and a half. The mason goes into the cut with a lamp on a stick and says the middle course is gone, not cracked, and that the hole has got a little wider every week for about eleven weeks, **and then says the thing the block needs: I am not laying the fourth course, and I want that said out loud today, because the fourth course wants the middle one back first, and there is nothing to lay a fourth course on, and that is not a reason and not a law, it is a mason telling nine people what is under his hand.** A man of about thirty-three asks then when, and the answer is one word, and it is not a date: when somebody pays for it. **Aren Kest carries buckets for two hours on his own account, is counted by the boy as one of the nine, and is told by the mason to go up the bank backwards with his left hand on a rope, and does.** The last act is the boy of about seventeen saying he is not going to be a mason, and the mason telling him why in his own trade, and the boy saying he will be at a sand yard on Thursday. **The fourth course is not laid, the mallet is not picked up, and nobody is scheduled to pick it up.** Nobody thanked anybody. Chapter body 3,606.

**`chapter-0687.md`, 3,431 words.** Frostmonth the sixth, a Saturday, a market morning, the second hour, the boards. **The coin that was not offered, named by somebody else.** The man of about fifty-one who owns a field on the low side of a cut a mile and a half off walks into a market street on purpose, having asked in Fallowmonth for the cost of a barrow drain to be said in a market street, and says the thing nine counties has and has not used, and it is not a figure and not a body of men and there is not a name at the top of it: **about nine people will get up and go to a gate and say no in the open in daylight where anybody going past can hear it, and write down where they were and what they said and the day, and leave the paper where a person can find it. Nobody has ever asked them to do it as a body, and the reason is that the asking is a head.** He reads the nine out loud by their trades and puts himself in the list. A woman of about sixty asks what it is worth if nobody can ask for it, and the answer is that it is worth what a barrow drain is worth, which is nothing until the day somebody needs it, and that this county has not got a lever, it has got nine people and a habit. **The copyist says there is nowhere in nine counties to put it, and will not rule a head for it.** A man of about thirty-four with a satchel says then ask them, and is refused, and the refusal is not argued with, and the badge-man says it in twenty-one words, that nobody asks and the nine turn up and he is not going to be thanked for any part of it. **A woman of about thirty-three who keeps a chandler's shop puts the price on it out loud, and it is the woman of about thirty-nine at a gate two days off, four households at a crossing nine miles off, a boy of about fourteen in no danger, and four people on a Friday at a cut who come out for nothing and are not thanked for it.** A household of about five says no to being on a list and is not argued with, and the woman of about twenty-four who asked admits she asked and says the reason was that she wanted to be in a thing. **The last act is a man of about thirty-three who sells cloth three doors up saying he has got nine names in his head and is not going to write them down, and four people asking him for the list and five not, and him writing nothing.** Nobody thanked anybody. Chapter body 3,101.

**`chapter-0688.md`, 3,429 words.** Frostmonth the eleventh, a Thursday, a market morning, the second hour in the market street and then the third hour at a door on a lane. **The nine questions and the tenth, and what a book is for.** The room is open because it is a market morning and about nine people have come in about a door on a lane between the third hour and the fifth hour, and every one of them came about a return. The press stands open and is not dated, the register has ten questions and no finding under any of the ten, the fourth head is empty, the day-book has eleven entries, and the drawer about eleven inches deep is shut and is not counted. **The copyist says what the room can do, which is hold a decision somebody can be brought to, refuse a thing and keep the refusal for four and a half years, and count, and then says all four of the things it cannot do, in one go, and the fourth is that it cannot be a person at the end of a road, which she says she has known since the middle of Longlight and has never said out loud in a room.** Then a woman of about fifty-one who keeps a lodging house four streets up, who has had about nine men of a forward party under her roof since the middle of Fallowmonth and who says she is not a clerk, says what a book is for: **a book is for the person who is in it and not for the person who keeps it, and every one of the four books she has read in this county in about four years is a book about about nine people, and not one of the four knew it was being written, and she fed about four of them while they were in it.** And then she says the whole of what the three series are for, which is that a county can say out loud that a person exists and can be produced, and that this is worth four and a half years and nobody in the room should be ashamed of it. **The copyist says no word at all for as long as it takes to turn a cart.** The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room says for the third time in four and a half years that the fourth head ought not to be there and is right and is not going to stop saying it, and is refused in six words. A man of about thirty-four with a satchel is given the register, squares it against the table edge the way she does, says he expected a finding and that an empty ruled head is the best thing in the room, and refuses to say why so that the next person through the door cannot work it out and write it down. **A man of about thirty-eight asks to leave nine boards in the room over the winter and is refused, and says it is the fourth thing he has been refused there in about four years and that he has come anyway each time.** Aren Kest is on the step at about the fourth hour beside the woman of about twenty-four and neither of them says anything. Nobody thanked anybody. Chapter body 3,144.

**`chapter-0689.md`, 3,597 words.** Frostmonth the thirteenth, a Saturday, a market morning, the second hour, the hearing in the street, about four hundred people and about two hundred stopped. **The captain's difficulty, and a man who is not a knave and is not called either.** `Halvard Rees`, a return and not a spend, comes up the market street on foot with the serjeant and about four men and no horse, and puts the sheet of counties on the boards himself, and says three things and then a fourth and then a fifth. **He has been at it since the eighth of Embermonth, has taken one road in one county at a time off a list of about thirty, has not read the last twenty-one, and has a serjeant somewhere every three days with a sheet and about four men and a copy of a line of his warrant and no warrant.** He says he has nine men on a road in this county and about nine he cannot spare, and says it a third time in front of four hundred people so that it will be written down that a captain of about forty-four has said out loud that he cannot spare his own men. **Then the load-bearing sentence: the instrument is not the difficulty, the shape is.** He says his paper is legal and is competent, and nobody in this county has ever said in public that it is not, and that it has no place in it, and that a warrant which names a place can be refused at a gate and this one says wherever the works are, and that a thing with no end in it is a thing a man cannot be wrong about, and that nobody can be argued with, including the people in it. He says he was wrong about a ford and a woman of about fifty told him so in the rain, that it is the only thing he has had in five weeks that anybody in this county could use against him, and that he would like nine more. A man of about thirty-four asks then what would you like us to do, and the answer is that he would like them to go on doing exactly what they are doing. **The woman of about thirty-six says that nobody in a coat has ever asked her for a name and that the captain has asked about a ford and a chain and whether a road may be shut, and that a man with a warrant is the only person in a coat who has never asked her for anything.** The badge-man says it in twenty-eight words, that the captain is the only man in nine counties who has never asked and that he has no clause for him and is not going to write one. **The last act is a disagreement at the low end of the boards in front of about two hundred people: the captain says leave a space on the page, the man of about thirty-one with the small book says a page has no space for an answer and that a space he puts in with a pencil in about four seconds he cannot take out in about four years, and the captain says you are right, and that he has been asking a man to invent a thing a page cannot hold for five weeks, and that the difficulty he came to that market street with is not a shape, it is a pencil.** Nobody thanked anybody. Chapter body 3,316.

**`chapter-0690.md`, 3,942 words.** Hearthmonth the first, a Wednesday, not a market morning, the first of a month, the room over the weigh-house shut behind its own door and the hearing not sitting. **The block's close and the hand-off, and a thing that happens.** About four people come to the boards out of habit and about five do not, and the badge-man says the shape of his own arrangement in one sentence, that a thing that stands in a street on the first of a month has no street on the first of a month nine times in twelve, and says he said it once already on the eighth of Wolfmonth and this is the ninth of those twelves. **All four of the standing things are named as standing: the nine hundred words under a cloth that cannot be signed and cannot be given away, the sixth clause wrong and unchanged and unanswered; the register with ten questions and no finding under any of them and a fourth head empty for about eleven months; the bar of wood in the grass at the foot of the lane by the Low Gate that nobody has lifted, nobody has scheduled lifting, and whose four questions are not going to be asked; and the fourth course of stone on the bank with the mallet on it, not laid, not picked up.** The chain is still on two posts, nine tons of stone is still in a low approach nine miles off, and a man of about forty-three with a yard has been going over that stone in about nine minutes a time for eleven weeks. A man of about thirty-four with a satchel says the finding of the block, that nine counties has got about nine people and no document, and that a thing with no document cannot be signed, cannot be given away and cannot be taken off anybody, and that this is not a failure but the shape, and that the worst thing about the arrangement is that it cannot be given away, and that he spent two months in Fallowmonth writing nine hundred words trying to give it away. **The woman of about thirty-one who keeps the smith's house asks for the third time in front of about four people whether any of this is for her, and the badge-man says no, and then says the other half, which is that if she is on the thing then she does not have to be found and he does not have to find her, and that is the whole of what he has and it is not nothing and it is not a place, and she says no out loud, kindly, because she does not want to be on a thing she cannot find.** **The small concrete local decision is not the protagonist's: a man of about sixty-two with a wain and a team of four walks the fourth and best of his team up the made road at about the sixth hour, puts it in a field four miles off where the forward party has not been, ties the gate, and says out loud that he has said yes to a man in a livery four times in about a month, that he is not going to say yes a fifth time and is not going to say no either, that he has watched four people say no this year and every one of them has had to explain it to somebody afterwards, that the horse is in this field and he is not going to say where or why, and that he loses the sand contract for Tuesdays, which is about nine days a year and the whole of what the field is for.** Nobody thanked anybody. Chapter body 3,682.

---

## Volume 14, Batch 0005 — Chapters 691 to 700, "The Crossings Held"

**`chapter-0691.md`, 3,909 words.** Hearthmonth the second, a Thursday and a market morning, the second hour in the market street at the boards, about four hundred people in it and about two hundred stopped, the first of nine days of held crossings. **The block's promise and the coin spent without being asked.** A man of about thirty-eight who puts boards on the inside of gates walks four miles in to account for a gate where nothing happened and then says he shut it on his own account with nobody's asking, and a man of about fifty-eight with a badge and no head says the whole of the procedure out loud in nine words, which is that nobody says anything to anybody and nobody is asked, and then says what it is not, which is five things refused in one breath including a name at the top of it. **The cost is named out loud by `Nell Drury`, a woman of thirty-three who keeps a chandler's shop at the low end of the boards, and the cost is that about four hundred people will walk past a thing they cannot name, and she says that is the arrangement working and says not one person is brave.** She refuses a book as well, because writing nine gates down is a thing a man can ask a shopkeeper for. The badge-man says the arrangement cannot report whether it worked and there will not be more of it. At the seventh hour a Crown cart comes up to the market town's own gate and the man in the livery asks which road goes up to the works and the gate does its ordinary work and nobody says anything to anybody. Nobody thanked anybody and nothing was signed.

**`chapter-0692.md`, 3,478 words.** Hearthmonth the fifth, a Sunday, not a market morning, the room over the weigh-house shut behind its own door and the hearing not sitting, the fifth of nine days. **The ferry city is not asked, and the decision is not the protagonist's.** Four people decline in turn to be the one who decides, fast, and a man of about fifty-two who keeps four miles under contract for about nineteen years agrees to decide it and says he has no standing in it and knows that better than the nine of them. **His reason is that a city four hundred miles off which has said it will name a place has put its name to nothing, that the day anybody asks it is the day it is in the taking, and that nine counties has never once had to refuse a thing a city said and the only way to keep on not having to is not to ask.** He refuses to have it put on the arrangement and puts his own words back on his own trade. Nobody names the city, nobody is sent, and the question of who sent anybody is asked and is not answered. The day-book stands at eleven entries where it stood on the first of Hearthmonth.

**`chapter-0693.md`, 3,621 words.** Hearthmonth the eleventh, a Saturday and a market morning, about four hundred people and about two hundred stopped. **A serjeant of about thirty-eight with a sheet and about four men and a copy of one line of a warrant and not the warrant, on a three-day interval since the middle of Fallowmonth, reads four miles of road out of a book.** He asks one question three times and gets no answer three times, and says out loud in the open that his own page will be the evidence against his own question in about four years, which is why he is called neither a knave nor a fool in this county. **The local people come one at a time on their own errands and are not asked anything, and four households who use that four miles say four ordinary things about a lane, including that a lane belongs to using and that nobody can prove a lane was his.** The man in the livery writes *no answer given*. Nobody thanked him and nobody gave him an answer.

**`chapter-0694.md`, 3,517 words.** Hearthmonth the fourteenth, a Tuesday, not a market morning, the room shut behind its own door. **A lawful sealed order with a name at the top of it requires eleven persons at a named crossing by the eighteenth, and nobody is killed and nobody is told anything.** Its arithmetic is on the page in about four hundred words: nine hundred and ninety-five on the roll, one in ninety, eleven written down and the remainder thrown away every quarter for four and a half years. **The man of about thirty-eight who keeps that gate obeys it by converting the figure into the fifth thing on the same form, which is labour, and chalks it on his own gate, and the Crown man writes eleven anyway so that a contradiction stands on the same gate on purpose.** The woman of about forty-nine who keeps four beds says the true thing, that a name can be refused and a road can be shut and a figure with a day on it cannot, and that it can be answered on Friday. **The antagonist's name is printed once in this block, as a document, in the middle of the sheet.** The badge-man says in twenty-four words that he has three clauses about people and roads and not one about a number.

**`chapter-0695.md`, 3,861 words.** Hearthmonth the eighteenth, a Saturday and a market morning, the second hour at the crossing where the lane off the made road takes the channel, sleet driving off the water, and nine minutes. **The volume's loudest page and its climax.** A cart bridge a foot down at the low end, propped since the middle of Longlight, nine men of that gate working a day they wrote in chalk, and a Crown cart that comes onto the deck and stops. **Aren Kest goes down on his knees on a wet plank deck with a bad leg and works a cracked sill beam back and forth a quarter of an inch at a time for about nine minutes, and the thing that actually stops the cart is a man of about forty-four with a wain who turns his team up a wet bank and backs it across a one-cart lane by sitting down on his own shafts, and the cart cannot get round a wain and cannot get past it.** The man in the livery asks once and does not ask twice and records a bridge going down as a fact about a deck in thirteen words. **The badge-man comes at the fourth hour and stands at the low end in the wet and does not go on it and says in thirty-eight words that he has no clause and has had none since the middle of Longlight, and he does not say one word about who did it and nobody asked him.** Nobody thanked anybody, nobody was hurt, and the nine men of that gate worked out all eleven days of it at a crossing that was not passable.

**`chapter-0696.md`, 3,503 words.** Hearthmonth the twenty-second, a Wednesday, about nine people in a market street with no market in it, four days after the bridge went down. **`Tovan Kest`, a carrier of about twenty-eight, has come four days on foot on his own account.** He refuses, out loud, in the open, to let a carter name the carriers, and gives the reason in his own trade: a path community is nine houses and a set of crossings with no head, nobody is told where to be, and nobody up that road had a reason to be at that crossing on the eighteenth. **Then he refuses the harder thing, which is that nobody will name the man who let the bridge go, and he does it by reading out a leaf dated the eleventh of Hearthmonth on which nine houses wrote that the roads on that coast shut nothing, and putting the date beside it, and saying that a deck is a thing anybody can look at and a man is not.** There is a second leaf, dated the ninth, that he will not read in that street because it is not addressed to him. Aren Kest stands at the high end of the boards through all of it and does not speak once and is not looked at, and about nine people in that street work out that the man reading the date was reading it for somebody. **The kin-term is printed nowhere and the relationship is the shape of the argument.**

**`chapter-0697.md`, 3,663 words.** Hearthmonth the twenty-fifth, a Saturday and a market morning, about four hundred people and about two hundred stopped, the largest crowd in the market town since the first of Embermonth. **The Gatehouse is exposed.** Four conversations four feet apart, none of them put together on purpose: a cloth seller stopped at the ninth mile stone because a Crown officer stood against a wall with his back to the road; a wain driver of about forty-four who has driven that road since he was about nine and has never once been through a gate in a wall; a beekeeper of about nineteen who has seen smoke off a chimney four days in seven since he was about eleven and has never asked what is behind the wall; and a yard keeper of about forty-three who sent a cart in nine years ago and was told not to ask and has thought about not asking once a week for nine years. **About nine people put four conversations together and get to about four and a half years, and nobody explains the mechanism and no character says what a roadmark is and the notebook's separate leaf is not looked at. No System panel is spent in this chapter.**

**`chapter-0698.md`, 3,471 words.** Hearthmonth the twenty-sixth, a Sunday, no market, the room shut behind its own door. **The practical limit of the stage is a person.** Three mornings: at the sixth mile stone, at the seventh mile wall-foot, and on this morning at about two hundred yards short of the shut gate. **His left foot will not go forward. He puts it forward twice, waits, tries a third time, and does not try a fourth, because a man who tries a thing nine times is a man who wants it to work. He puts his stick down in wet grass in order to try to walk two hundred yards.** He walks back and stays in, and nobody is angry with him and nobody has asked him about it, and about nine people know and about nine counties have not been told. **The woman of about fifty-one who keeps a lodging house comes down a lane and says the true thing about a man who cannot come, and it is not comfort: a bed is for a person who has come off a road, there is not a bed for a person who has not been able to come off a road, and those are two different people, and there is nothing in her trade that fills a bed because a man does not go.** On the Monday he goes up the road at about the ninth hour as he has every morning since the first of Longlight.

**`chapter-0699.md`, 3,669 words.** Goatmonth the first, a Friday, not a market morning, the first of a month, the room shut and the boards standing in an empty street, about nine people in it and nothing at the top of it. **The resolution.** Three people say the whole of what happened and the badge-man says nothing at all for two hours, which nine people say is the hardest thing he has ever done, and about nine people come and about five do not. **He says nine words and it is the last thing anybody says in that street that day, which is that nothing happens on the first of the next month, and then ten words, which are that nobody can call the next meeting and nobody needs to, because the thing that wanted a meeting was a man with a warrant and he has stopped.** The captain is on a made road four days off and is not in that street, the woman of about thirty-nine is at her gate two days off with it open, the delegation got home, and the city four hundred miles off has not been asked and nobody is being sent to it. **The march came into nine counties and went out of it and took no road and not one person has ever said the word for that, and the exposure of the wall is a worse thing for every person in nine counties than a wall four hundred people walked past, and about four people have said out loud that they would give the whole of four and a half years back to have it shut again and about five have said exactly the same thing.** The hearing certifies nothing in fourteen words. The last beat is small and local and not the protagonist's: a chandler of thirty-three puts a cloth over a bench she has put out of her shop every market morning for eleven years with nothing on it.

**`chapter-0700.md`, 2,701 words.** Goatmonth the twenty-fifth, a Monday, not a market morning, the last day of the volume, a man four days off on this made road with a stick and on his own. **The volume's last main-world page, and it is not a settlement.** He walks the four days, past the ninth mile stone and the lane with the weigh-house in it four hundred paces from a door he has gone past since the first of Longlight, and does not go up the lane. **A man of about forty-one who keeps a drove and a byre shuts a gate across a lane that goes over the shoulder of the hill, and when asked how old the lane is, says ten words, which are that it was a road before this county had a name, and has known it for about eleven years and said nothing about it.** Nobody establishes whether the second question went unasked because he had the answer or because he had worked out that a man who says ten words at a gate has said all ten on purpose. He walks on to the top of the stone causeway and stands with his stick against the parapet. **A lamp is lit at the gate at four days on the coping every Monday afternoon whether anybody is there or not, and on the gate post above it there is a hand bell that is rung when the water is over the causeway, and it has not been rung in this weather at all, and then a bell answers across the water, and he had not rung it.**

---

## Volume 15, Batch 0001 — Chapters 701 to 710, "What Is at the Top of the Lane"

**`chapter-0701.md`, 3,765 words.** Goatmonth the twenty-sixth, YR 321, a Tuesday, not a market morning, the room over the weigh-house shut behind its own door, four days out on the made road at the head of it where the water is. **The block's gift and the last page of Volume 14 answered cheaply and not explained.** The top of a tide in Goatmonth is the highest it comes hereabouts all year; the channel is nine hundred yards wide; the causeway is nine hundred paces with a parapet a man can lean on. **The man who keeps that gate rings the hand bell because the water is over the causeway, nine times, and about four people in nine counties say he has rung it about nine hundred times in about eleven years and about five say nobody has asked him.** The far side cannot be seen, which has not happened in about four and a half years. He waits four hours, reads the drop off a seam in the coping rather than a board, packs the wet out of his boot, crosses in about nine minutes, stands on the far bank about as long as it takes to turn a cart twice over, **and finds a made road going north with about nine hundred yards of laid kerb on both sides and then grass, and a shut hut four hundred yards past the end of the kerb with driftwood against it.** He does not ask the gate keeper a single question about the far bank, and nobody in nine counties has ever been able to establish whether that was because he had the answer already or because he had worked out in about four seconds that a man who has rung a bell nine hundred times has got nothing else to give anybody. He comes back with salt on the front of his coat, cleans the eleventh mile stone, looks back down four days of road for the first time in about four and a half years, tells nobody, is not asked, and a woman of about forty-nine who keeps a kitchen and four beds asks him where he has been on the step and he says the north end. Nobody thanked anybody.

**`chapter-0702.md`, 3,673 words.** Goatmonth the twenty-eighth, YR 321, a Thursday and a market morning, about four hundred people and about two hundred stopped, the room open. **The question, and the refusal to send anybody.** The man of about thirty-three who sells cloth three doors up asks, out loud, at the boards, what is on the far side of the water, and takes his own cloth off his forearm first. Nobody answers it. **The badge-man comes down the lane at a slow walk, puts his hand flat on the wet rail, looks at the sea end of the street, and says the whole of it in fifty words: nobody in nine counties has been across that water and come back on foot in about eleven years except the man who keeps that gate, and he is not going to send anybody, and the reason is that sending is making a head.** He then says the half of it that four people had not understood, which is that a man who goes is not a head and a man who sends is a head, and that if anybody wants to go then that is his own account and not this county's business. **The woman of about thirty-nine who keeps the second gate from the top of the made road then puts the leaf of the eleventh of Hearthmonth on the market street and says the part that is her own and not the houses', which is that a house that writes no down has said it once and it stands and a gate that is shut has to be shut again by somebody, and she has said no four times this month.** The ordinary half follows: the wheel off a cart, a boy with a pail, two women and a bolt of cloth, a chalked price at the inn, the weigh-house and about four hundred and nine parish rolls a month. **The man of thirty-two is at the sea end of the street in a coat with salt on it and is asked nothing and says nothing, and about four people have worked out that he was on the far bank on Tuesday and about five have not.** Nobody thanked anybody.

**`chapter-0703.md`, 3,565 words.** Thawmonth the first, YR 322, a Sunday, not a market morning, **the first of a month and the first day of a new year**, the bolt still on the door from the Saturday, about four people in the market street and nothing at the top of it. **The volume's engine is a thing with nowhere to go.** About ten firsts of a month come in the ten months from here and about two of them have a market on them; the other eight come into an empty street. The badge-man is not there and nobody sent for him and nobody sent anybody. **The man of thirty-two comes fifth, has a question, and does not put it anywhere, because there is no register to put it in and no bench to say it at and no copyist to write it down, and the register holds ten questions with no finding under any of them and the fourth ruled head has been empty for about twelve months.** He says eighteen words out loud and does not go and find anybody. **He walks down the four hundred paces of the lane, stops at about the thirteenth pace at a gutter with about nine days of grit in the ice in it, goes on to her plank door, does not knock and does not put his hand on it, stands on a stone step with a wear in the middle of it for about as long as it takes to turn a cart, and goes back up the lane.** Four people in that market street had worked out that the man who goes had come fifth to a thing that does not happen. The room stays bolted. Nobody wrote it down.

**`chapter-0704.md`, 3,304 words.** Thawmonth the fifth, YR 322, a Thursday and a market morning four days off, the fourth hour at the head of the made road, a five-bar gate across a lane. **`Orme Skell`, forty-one, keeps a drove, a byre and about nine hundred yards of stone wall, and his name is spent here.** The bar goes in on the lane side, which is shutting the gate against the road and not against himself. The drove is nine two-year-old beasts and it goes out on Thursdays through a gap in the wall about nine feet wide which is not a gate and has never been one, and it goes up the lane about nine hundred yards and stands in a field with a stone wall round it, **and that is the whole of what keeps the lane a lane.** **The protagonist does not ask a second question and says why out loud in his own trade: he has asked nine hundred girders the same question twice, and you do not ask a man who keeps beasts about a road twice, because the first answer is the whole of what he has, and a second question makes it a thing he has been asked to produce, and then it is a thing with a use in it.** Orme Skell opens the gate on his own account for about as long as it takes to shut a cart, gives no reason anybody in nine counties can repeat, **says fourteen words, that a gate nobody has looked at is not a gate but a story, and corrects the county's four-and-a-half-year figure to about eleven years, and nobody argues with him because it was his gate.** Nobody goes up the lane. Nobody thanked either of them.

**`chapter-0705.md`, 3,154 words.** Thawmonth the ninth, YR 322, a Monday, not a market morning, the room shut, four days off this market town at the head of that made road and about half a mile below the wall on the low side, about nine hives in a walled yard. **`Dav Rundle`, nineteen, keeps bees and has kept them since he was about eleven, and his name is spent here.** The yard is four rods by six with a stone wall and a beam on two blocks of stone that is the oldest kind of bee bench there is. He counts nine frames of brood twice, which is the number he counted in the middle of Frostmonth and in the middle of Longlight. **A late frost came on the night of the eighth and takes nine frames, and he knew two days before anybody else because the bees were flying about four feet off the ground and coming back with nothing.** Nine frames is about a fifth of a good year and about nine days of not eating enough, and it is nobody else's business and does not have to be anybody's fault. He says out loud that he is going up the shoulder on the Thursday and why, in twenty-two words: **the broom is on that shoulder and it is on nowhere else, and I want it, and it is mine to want.** He is three weeks early. Nobody sent him and nobody asked him and nobody has ever asked him a question.

**`chapter-0706.md`, 3,338 words.** Thawmonth the twelfth, YR 322, a Thursday and a market morning four days off, the second hour at the head of the made road, nine beasts going out through a gap in a wall and a young man going up a lane with them on his own account. **The oldest road anybody in nine counties knows of, and what is at the top of it.** The lane is about two miles, is not made and never has been, is nine feet wide because a drove is nine feet wide, and turns twice before the shoulder. Past the turns there are **about nine hundred yards of road kerbed on both sides in the same stone as the wall at the bottom of the lane, and then the kerb stops and the road goes on as a line nine feet wide in the grass for about nine hundred yards.** The broom is not on and it is about three weeks early. **At the far end of the line there are nine stones set across it, about the height of a knee, with the tops of them all at the same height**, and he puts his hand on the first of them and does not go past it, and he sits on the ninth and looks back at nine hundred yards of kerb. He is about nine hundred yards short of the broom. At about the ninth hour he hears one pair of wheels on a made surface on the other side of the nine stones, once, and it goes away and does not come back, **and he writes nothing down and had nothing to write it on.** He comes down the lane at the eleventh hour with nothing, and a boy of about eleven who keeps goats asks him, on the lane, whether there is anything up there.

**`chapter-0707.md`, 2,823 words.** Thawmonth the nineteenth, YR 322, a Thursday and a market morning, about four hundred people and about two hundred stopped, **the largest crowd in that market town since the twenty-fifth of Hearthmonth.** `Dav Rundle` says the four things in the open in his own order and not anybody's order: two miles of unmade lane turning twice, nine hundred yards of kerbed road, nine hundred yards of a line in the grass with every yard of it visible from the end, nine stones at the same height, and one cart heard once at the ninth hour that went away and did not come back. **A man of about twenty-six begins to number them and gets as far as the second number and about nine people in that street say the same word at the same moment and he stops, and nobody has ever established whose word it was.** The young man then says he is not going to be asked about it again that day. **The man of about fifty-two who keeps four miles of made road under contract refuses, out loud, in the open, in his own trade: he is not going up that lane, he has about nine men on four miles and about nine he cannot spare, and four miles in Mudmonth is about nine hundred yards of mud and nobody has told him it is nobody's business.** The badge-man says in thirty-two words that a thing said in this street is worth less than a thing said in a trade, and does not answer how anybody finds out whether it is true. About five of the four hundred say out loud that they believe it. Nobody wrote it down.

**`chapter-0708.md`, 2,742 words.** Thawmonth the twenty-sixth, YR 322, a Thursday and a market morning, about four hundred people and about two hundred stopped, the room open. **The protagonist's age is reconciled and thirty-two stands.** A Crown party's cart went up the made road in the middle of Hearthmonth with paper in a handbasket and a cloth on top, and the same basket went into the hired room over a saddler's, and the page reached the room over the weigh-house about a fortnight ago and was not entered in the day-book. **The copyist of about twenty-six comes down the thirteen stairs once, does not go back up, and reads out the fen county road office roll of hands, dated YR 312, which says that Aren Kest was entered at the age of twenty-nine years, having served seven years before he was entered.** Twenty-nine in YR 312 is thirty-eight in YR 322. Four people in that market street do the sum in about nine seconds and about five do not, and **the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps that room is not in the lane and not at the stairs and nobody sent for her and he did not go up the lane.** He says the true thing out loud and then stops: **a paper in this county is wrong about him, and it is wrong in the direction that makes him more use to somebody, and a man of thirty-eight with a master's entry behind him is a man somebody can put at a gate and a man of thirty-two is not.** Nobody writes it down and nobody finds out who asked the fen county road office for the page, and the asking is not traced. The copyist's last sentence is eighteen words and she says them to the leaf. Nobody thanked him, nobody was angry with him, nobody argued with him and nobody defended him.

**`chapter-0709.md`, 3,173 words.** Mudmonth the first, YR 322, a Tuesday, not a market morning, the first of a month, the room shut behind its own door, about four people in the market street. **The volume's own discovery turns out to be un-actionable and Mudmonth is why.** About four people come and about five do not and the badge-man is not there for the third time running. **The water came up in the lane at the head of the made road on the twenty-fourth of Thawmonth and has been under since, which is seven days, and the shoulder above it does not go under.** A man of about thirty-eight who puts boards on the inside of gates says out loud that a gate at the end of a lane that is under is not shut and is not open and is the third thing, and that nobody has a name for the third thing. **The one decision is not the protagonist's: the man who keeps four miles under contract says he is not going up the lane before Hearthmonth and gives the reason in his trade, and that is the first date anybody in nine counties has given for anything since the first of Longlight.** The weigh-house figure is about nine hundred sheets a month in three series and a roll of hands is not a sheet, which is the first time anybody has noticed the room's practice has a blind spot in the shape of a person. The cloth seller still has nine names in his head and has still not written them down, and about four people asked for the list in Frostmonth and about five did not. The lane goes under about twice a year and about four people in that street on that morning said out loud that they had not known that either.

**`chapter-0710.md`, 2,867 words.** Mudmonth the twenty-fifth, YR 322, a Friday, not a market morning, the room shut and the hearing not sitting, four days out at the head of a made road where nine yards of a wall is down. **The block's close, and it is a hand-off and not a settlement.** Nine yards of the nine hundred yards came down on the twenty-eighth of Thawmonth and the water took the foot of it out on the second of Mudmonth. **The man of about forty-one has been rebuilding it alone, two miles to the quarry, nine loads, about nine days.** At the tenth hour the man of thirty-two comes up the made road on foot, does not ask to help, and the only question asked on that wall in about an hour is whether the bar is wanted. **The one thing either of them says in about an hour is sixteen words: you can have the bar from the second yard on there and not off this end.** The gate has been shut since the sixth of Thawmonth and is shut, and about four people say that a gate shut against a lane that is under is a gate shut against nothing. **The drove went up the lane alone on the Thursday for the first time in about eleven years.** And the protagonist asks a question out loud, to about one person, on a Friday afternoon in Mudmonth, that nobody answers and that he does not ask twice: **somebody with no standing in this county and no trade to lose has to go up that lane in Hearthmonth, and the whole of what he has to decide before then is whether he is that person or whether he is the man who keeps finding out what other people decide.** Nobody thanked anybody.

## Volume 15, Batch 0002 — Chapters 711 to 720, "Two of the Nine"

**`chapter-0711.md`, 3,365 words.** Mudmonth the twenty-sixth, YR 322, a Saturday and a market morning, about four hundred people and about two hundred stopped, the room open. **The block's opening, and the fact that turns it.** Forty-eight days after a late frost a young man of about nineteen keeps **one frame of brood where he had nine**, because a brood frame comes back in about four weeks in a year like this one, and this is the only number in nine counties anybody can check and he has nobody in nine counties to give it to. The drove went up the lane on the Thursday under nine inches of water and came back and nobody went with it, which has happened about nine times since the second of Mudmonth and never in about eleven years before that. Nine yards of a wall is still down and the man who keeps it has been carrying stone two miles for about thirty-one days alone. **In the room over the weigh-house, on a market morning, the man of thirty-two goes up the thirteen stairs for the first time anybody in nine counties has seen him in that room in about four and a half years, and there are two people in it, and neither of them says anything to him and he says nothing to either of them, and he looks at a press standing open with no date on it and comes down.** Then the man who keeps four miles of made road says out loud, in thirty-six words, in the open, that **the shoulder came up on the nineteenth and the lane is under to the second gate, and there has been nine days this month when a man could have stood up there and not found out**, and nobody stops him and nobody asks him a second question. Somebody asks out loud, kindly, whether anybody has tried to get to it in those nine days. Nobody thanked anybody.

**`chapter-0712.md`, 3,383 words.** Mudmonth the twenty-ninth, YR 322, a Tuesday and not a market morning, the room shut behind its own door, the hearing not sitting, four days out at the head of the made road. **The lane came out of the water the day before, after thirty-four days, and the third thing stopped being the third thing at about the fourth hour on the twenty-eighth of Mudmonth and nobody in nine counties noticed.** That is the chapter's finding and it is a flat one. The woman of about thirty-nine who keeps the second gate from the top of that made road takes water up to a house with no other water, does not put a pail inside her own gateway, and says the thing out loud, in forty-two words, in her own trade: **a gate is shut against a lane and a lane is a road, and I have kept a gate shut against that lane for as long as I have kept it and the water was never in it once until this year.** The hives, the boy of about eleven who keeps goats at the gap asking out loud whether it has gone down yet, the man of about forty-one laying stone and not looking up, and a man of thirty-two who stands at the mouth of a lane that is not under for the first time in thirty-four days and looks up about nine hundred yards of it and does not go up it. Nobody thanked anybody.

**`chapter-0713.md`, 3,569 words.** Longlight the first, YR 322, a Saturday and a market morning and the first of a month, about four hundred people, not one of them at the boards. **The gift, and what a party is.** **The first of a month in this county comes whether or not anybody is in the street for it, and this is the first one in about four and a half years to arrive with four hundred people standing about nine feet off it.** Longlight is the month a boundary oath-stone is renewed in this world, the badge-man says so in eight words, and the cloth seller answers in twenty-four words that **there is no oath-stone in this county and there is no boundary on that made road to renew, and a month is a month**, and nobody argues with him and nobody agrees with him. **Five reasons in five trades, each out loud in the open:** four miles of made road come off that shoulder and the man who keeps them under contract for about nineteen years has never once been on the top of it, thirty words; every board a man puts on the inside of a gate goes on a gate somebody can stand in front of, fifty words; about nine hundred yards of kerb on a far bank and then grass and a man who keeps eels two days north of it has never crossed on foot, forty-four words; a gate keeper's reason, reported and not numbered; and a man with a satchel who is the mouth that says the finding of things and has never had one checked. **Then the badge-man prices the party itself in fifty-six words: there are five people going up a lane on their own business and there is no head of it and there is no paper of it, and sending one of them would make a head, and nobody in this street has sent anybody.** The man of thirty-two is the sixth and is not one of the five, and about four people counted five and about five counted six and nobody said so out loud. Nobody thanked anybody.

**`chapter-0714.md`, 3,597 words.** Longlight the seventh, YR 322, a Friday and not a market morning, two days out on a drove road and up over a shoulder, six people on a shoulder with no head and no paper. **The first day on the shoulder.** They go through the gap at the low end of about nine hundred yards of stone wall, which is four feet high and about nine feet wide and is the width of a drove and is the only opening anybody has ever made in that wall in about eleven years, and the man of about forty-one stands at the head of it and does not help and does not watch them go and goes back in. The lane turns twice. The broom is not on and comes on about the fourth of Rainmonth. **The roadkeeper says the true thing about his own trade in forty-two words: four miles of made road come off that shoulder and this is the top of it, and I have laid stone on the low side of a hill for about nineteen years and I have never once laid stone on the flat.** About nine hundred yards of road kerbed on both sides, then about nine hundred yards of a line nine feet wide in the grass, then nine stones at the same height at the end of it, and **nobody goes past the ninth of the nine.** The far side is described in twenty-three words and not one word more: flat and low, a fence line, and beasts. The man with a satchel asks out loud how many people in nine counties have ever stood at the far end of that line, and the answer is one and he is the one. The gate keeper asks the man of thirty-two, in thirty-nine words, what his trade is, because he is the only one of the six with no trade to put at risk, and he answers in twenty-seven words: **I walk that road.** Nobody thanked anybody.

**`chapter-0715.md`, 3,441 words.** Longlight the eighth, YR 322, a Saturday and a market morning, about four hundred people and about two hundred stopped, the room open. **Eight days in which nothing has happened.** The middle of that morning happens in nine trades at about nine hundred people doing nine things at once, and four people in that street count nine trades at work at the same time and five count eight, and the ninth is the man who keeps the weigh-house and he is inside. **THE FILE CARRIES TWO PARAGRAPHS OF NEW SCENE PROSE WRITTEN IN THE SECOND REPAIR PASS, being a farrier at the second door, a barrow of onions and a stirrup leather mended against a wall, because the first pass removed the paragraph that had illustrated that claim as duplicated prose and left the claim standing on nothing; the restoration was deliberately not a restoration, because the paragraph that came out was the one hundred and four word run shared with two other files. The prose spends no name and prints no counted figure, and the file's ten counted speeches all still foot.** A fact said out loud on the first of a month has one life and the life is a week. The badge-man is not in the street and nobody sent for him. The hearing cannot be called and does not sit and its first of a month came on a Saturday with four hundred people about nine feet off it and not one of them at it, which is the fourth first of a month running and nobody has sent for the man who built it on any of them. The cloth seller asks, out loud, kindly, at the low end of the boards, **when do they come back down**, and nobody answers it, and it is the second question anybody in nine counties has asked about that lane and the first was a boy of about eleven who keeps goats asking whether there was anything up there. The nine names in the cloth seller's head from the middle of Fallowmonth are still not written down and this is the third refusal of nine names in a market street in this county and all three were made by two people in two trades. The weigh-house and about four hundred and nine parish rolls a month, and the good news, which is that the last month was the best month that weigh-house has had since the middle of Harvestmonth, and it is about sheets. Nobody thanked anybody.

**`chapter-0716.md`, 3,414 words.** Longlight the eleventh, YR 322, a Tuesday and not a market morning, five days up on a shoulder. **The test, and the one name.** **Two of the nine households that keep beasts on the flat past the nine stones came up to the line of them on the ninth of Longlight and one outsider came with them and the other five stayed nine hundred yards back at the end of the line.** **Seven of the nine did not come once and were asked nothing, and the reason is a rota of beasts kept about nine years and not broken once in the nine years: seven of the nine had stock on the flat in the week of the eleventh of Longlight and two did not, and a household with beasts on a flat in the middle of a drying month cannot be off that flat for a morning and cannot do it for a week.** **The man of about fifty-four who keeps the far end of that lane says it out loud in thirty-nine words and finishes with anybody who says it is fear or manners is not keeping beasts.** The person of about forty-four whose household keeps sheep says it for herself in forty-five words; the second of the two says it in thirty-one words, and the first then says in thirty words why the outsider is wanted at a line of stones: **you are not one of us and you have not got a beast on that flat and that is the whole of what I want at a line of stones.** **`Tarr Lyster`, the name, is spent here, once.** Nobody crosses the nine stones and nothing on the other side of them is seen from this side of them. Nobody thanked anybody.

**`chapter-0717.md`, 3,466 words.** Longlight the thirteenth, YR 322, a Thursday and a market morning four days off with no market on the made road, the ninth hour on a clear day, nine people on a shoulder. **The hearing of the far side, once, and only once.** Nine people on that shoulder is five who came up on the fifth, a man of thirty-two who is not one of the five, two households and a boundary keeper, and it is the largest number of people that has ever been on it. **At about the ninth hour, on a clear day with the wind dropped, three strokes of a thing being struck on something hard came from about four hundred yards off and went to about nine hundred yards off and did not come again.** It is not the cart of the twelfth of Thawmonth and the two are not the same sound. **The man of about thirty-four with a satchel says it out loud, in the open, in his own trade, in fifty-five words: a thing heard once is not a road and it is not a place and it is not a fact anybody can build on, and he says things out loud in a market street and has said them for four and a half years and nobody has ever built anything on one of them. Nobody agreed with him and nobody argued with him and he said nothing else that day.** The broom is not on and there are about twenty-one days to it. The frost on the morning of the thirteenth put a white line on the tops of the nine stones and nothing else on that shoulder. A person of about forty-four asks out loud where the three strokes came from, and the only answer anybody has is that it came from four hundred yards off and went to nine hundred. Nobody thanked anybody.

**`chapter-0718.md`, 3,327 words.** Longlight the seventeenth, YR 322, a Monday and not a market morning, the room shut and the hearing not sitting, eleven days on a shoulder, nine people on it. **The cost, and it is a crossing that is not there.** The hives stand at three frames of brood where there were nine, at ninety-nine days, and that is the first figure a person in nine counties has given this year that got better. **The man of thirty-two says it out loud, in the open, at about the eleventh hour, once, and it is fifty-four words: there is no crossing at these nine stones and there is not going to be one from this side, and the whole of what this party came up here to do cannot be done from this side, and I have got no standing in this county to say so and I am saying it. Nobody argued with him and nobody thanked him.** The person of about forty-four then says the other half out loud, in her own trade, that the crossing is not what nine households are for and there is no rota for it and nobody in nine counties would ever agree to it. **The eleven days are then counted out in five trades by the five themselves: four miles of made road a month short of a contractor, nine yards of boards not going on gates for about a fortnight, a gate left and not shut, thirteen days of a mouth that says things not saying them, and the fifth man refusing in the open to give his own cost and nobody asking him.** They carried water two hundred yards every morning and lit no fire at all. Nobody thanked anybody.

**`chapter-0719.md`, 3,134 words.** Longlight the nineteenth, YR 322, a Wednesday and not a market morning, one day after six people came down two miles of unmade road, four days off at the head of that made road. **The party comes down the lane and goes five ways.** Two days back is the whole distance and the first mile of it is always the slower one and nobody has ever worked out why. **Two of the nine households have been up to a line of nine stones and have not been past it and seven have not come once and were asked nothing, and the reason is a rota of beasts kept about nine years, and this is the first time anybody in this county has given a reason for not doing a thing that anybody with a beast could check in about nine seconds.** The roadkeeper goes back to his four miles the day he gets down and not the day after; the boarder goes toward the coast road with about nine yards of boards not going anywhere; the eel-man goes north with his hands split at the second joint of two fingers; the gate keeper shuts her own gate and puts her own bar in and stands in her own gateway, which is the difference between a gate shut by its keeper and a gate left; and the man with a satchel goes into the town and says nothing. The room over the weigh-house is shut and he does not go up the lane. A person on the made road asks him out loud, kindly, **does it go any further than that**, and he says it goes about nine hundred yards of kerb and about nine hundred yards of a line in the grass and stops at nine stones, and nobody asks him a second question, and **about four people in nine counties think the whole of this county's business is finished and about five think it is not.** Nobody thanked anybody.

**`chapter-0720.md`, 3,313 words.** Longlight the twenty-second, YR 322, a Saturday and a market morning, about four hundred people and about two hundred stopped, the room open. **The block's last page, and it is not the shoulder and not the stones.** The man of about fifty-eight with a badge and no head puts his hand flat on the wet rail and says the true thing about what a hearing is worth in forty-seven words: **a hearing is worth the paper it makes and this one has made nothing in four and a half years, and what it made is worth what the Marshal's hand makes of it, which is nothing, and that is the whole of what a hearing is worth.** **The man of about thirty-three who sells cloth three doors up takes his cloth off his forearm before he answers, as nobody in that street has ever seen him do, and answers in his own trade in sixty-six words: four hundred who have heard are not in the same place as four hundred who have not, because a person who has heard a thing can be found and a person who has not heard a thing cannot be found, and there is no other difference between the two of them and there never was.** The badge-man answers in forty-seven words that four hundred who have heard cannot be asked, and the reason has not changed since the first of Goatmonth, and it is that asking is making a head, and nobody has ever asked him what a head is for. **They disagree in the open and nobody agrees with either of them and nobody takes a side and nobody thanks anybody.** A man with a satchel asks out loud, kindly, what the four hundred would have to hear, and nobody answers it. Then the last of it, at the sea end of that street: **the question is whether four hundred people who have all heard the same four things are in a different position from four hundred people who have not, and he worked it out at about the tenth hour and did not say it out loud, and the reason he did not is that asking it would be sending, and a man who asks a market street whether four hundred people are a thing has made four hundred people into a thing.** Nobody thanked anybody.

## Volume 15, Batch 0003 — Chapters 721 to 730, "The Road That Stops Being Made"

**`chapter-0721.md`, 3,329 words.** Longlight the twenty-seventh, YR 322, a Thursday and a market morning, about four hundred people and about two hundred stopped, the room open. **Two accounts of one road put in front of each other for the first time in about four and a half years.** The man of about thirty-three who sells cloth three doors up takes the blue wool off his own forearm first, which nobody in that street has seen him do twice, the second time in the whole of the volume after Chapter 0702, and says out loud in fifty-five words that a road that goes between two places has both of the places under the grass and that he has sold cloth into nine counties for nine years and nobody has ever come into his shop and asked him where either of them is. **About an hour later and not in answer, the man of about fifty-two who keeps four miles of made road under contract for about nineteen years puts his hand flat on nine feet of a new kerb standing against a shop wall and says sixty-six words: a road that goes across a shoulder and does not go off it is a road that goes to a place that is not there any more, and he can tell you the two ends of a road that goes to a place and both of his are still standing.** The cloth seller answers in forty-four words, **then it is not a road, it is a lane**, and the roadkeeper says one word back, which is no. **The man of about thirty-four with a satchel then says out loud, in forty-seven words, that nobody in nine counties can say where the kerb stops, that one person has stood where it stops and has not written it down and has not said it, and that the rest of us have been about four and a half years getting used to not having it.** A question about a broom is asked kindly and the cloth seller refuses to be asked about one twice in the same street. The drove went out at the sixth hour, six of nine yards of wall are back in, and the hives stand at three frames of brood where there were nine.

**`chapter-0722.md`, 3,423 words.** Longlight the twenty-ninth, YR 322, a Saturday and a market morning, about four hundred people and about two hundred stopped, the room open. **The eighth question anybody in nine counties has asked about that lane, and the fourth nobody could answer, and it is the first one asked where about four hundred people could hear it.** The market-morning frame is stated here without an about in front of the figures for the first time in the block, on the ground that a man standing at the low end of those boards can count the ones who stop and get the same number a second time. The man of about thirty-four with a satchel says six words out loud: **Then where does the kerb stop?** Nobody answers and nobody tries. **The cloth seller refuses it out loud in forty words: a man who sells cloth is the last man in nine counties anybody should ask where a road stops, and I answered that question wrongly on Thursday and I am not going to answer it twice in the same street.** The roadkeeper says nothing.
